Top-Down or Bottom-Up? ( Full Version )
Thien Udomsrirungruang, Nobuko Yoshida

Abstract
. Multiparty session types (MPST) provide a type discipline for ensuring communication safety, deadlock-freedom and liveness for multiple concurrently running participants. The original formulation of MPST takes the top-down approach, where a global type specifies a bird’s eye view of the intended interactions between participants, and each distributed process is locally type-checked against its end-point projection. A more recent one takes the bottom-up approach, where a desired property φ \varphi of a set of participants is ensured if the same property φ \varphi is true for an ensemble of end-point types (a typing context) inferred from each participant. This paper compares these two main procedures of MPST, giving their detailed complexity analyses. To this aim, we build several new algorithms missing from the bottom-up or top-down workflows by using graph representation of session types ( type graphs ). We first propose a subtyping system based on type graphs, offering more efficient (quadratic) subtype-checking than the existing (exponential) inductive algorithm ( Ghilezan et al., 2019 ) . Next for the top-down, we measure complexity of the four end-point projections from the literature ( Honda et al., 2008 ; Yoshida and Hou, 2024 ; Tirore et al., 2023 ; Ghilezan et al., 2019 ) . For the coinductive projection in ( Ghilezan et al., 2019 ) , we build a new sound and complete PSPACE -algorithm using type graphs. For bottom-up, we develop a novel type inference system from MPST processes which generates minimum type graphs , succinctly capturing covariance of internal choice and contravariance of external choice. For property-checking of typing contexts, we achieve PSPACE -hardness by reducing it from the quantified Boolean formula (QBF) problem, and build nondeterministic algorithms that search for counterexamples to prove membership in PSPACE . We also present deterministic analogues of these algorithms that run in exponential time. Finally, we calculate the total complexity of the top-down and the bottom-up approaches. Our analyses reveal that the top-down based on global types is more efficient than the bottom-up in many realistic cases; liveness checking for typing contexts in the bottom-up has the highest complexity; and the type inference costs exponential against the size of a process, which impacts the total complexity.

Complexity Analyses of Synchronous Multiparty Session Types

1.
Introduction

With the growing complexity of communication behaviours
of distributed and concurrent systems,
their protocol specification has become of central
importance
(
Kuhn et al., 2023
;
Montesi, 2023
)
.

Multiparty session types
(MPST)
(
Honda et al., 2008
)

offer a type discipline
for ensuring desirable properties such as deadlock-freedom
and liveness for multiple interacting participants
with guidance by a protocol specification, called
global types
.
A global type gives a system designer
an entire interaction scenario.
It is projected into a collection of
end-point
or
local types
, and
each participant is type-checked locally and independently
against its local type.
This can ensure that the execution among participants
does not get stuck, following a protocol, offering
correctness by construction
.
This approach, called
top-down
, has
been implemented and integrated into diverse tools and
programming languages. The theory of the end-point
projections has been advanced over the years based on
a variety of
techniques from e.g., event structures,
communicating
automata, linear and separation logics,
message sequence charts and algebraic rewriting

(
Castellani et al., 2023
;
Majumdar et al., 2021
;
Li et al., 2024b
;
Li et al., 2024a
;
Stutz, 2023
;
Jongmans and Yoshida, 2020
;
Castro-Perez and Yoshida, 2020
;
Barbanera et al., 2023
)
, and
is mechanised by several tools
(
Tirore et al., 2023
;
Jacobs et al., 2022
;
Castro-Perez et al., 2021
)
.

{\sf G}

{\sf T}_{2}

Figure 1
.
Overview of the top-down and bottom-up procedures.

Motivated by a growth of model checking tools,
Scalas and Yoshida
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
proposed a general MPST
typing system which does
not
require global types:
it can ensure desired properties of processes
by checking the same properties of typing contexts (a collection of
local types).
They give specifications for these properties in modal

\mu
-calculus formulae checkable by the mCRL2 checker
(
Bunte et al., 2019
)
.
Their typability is not limited by
projectability or
implementability of
global types,
giving a
complete
set of processes
which satisfy the desired properties.
We call their framework,
bottom-up
.

This paper
gives
detailed and comprehensive complexity analyses of the
top-down
and

bottom-up
procedures developed in
various MPST literature.
Our motivation is very simple:
the top-down approach
gives often less typable processes
than the bottom-up, but it is
useful in practice and believed to be more tractable.
Can we measure and compare the two approaches precisely?

Figure
1(a)
gives a workflow of the top-down approach:
first, we project a global type
{\sf G}
to a set of
local types
\{{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
(Step (i)).
Each process
P_{i}
is then type-checked against a subtype
{\sf T}_{i}^{\prime}
of

{\sf T}_{i}
such that
{\sf T}_{i}\leqslant{\sf T}_{i}^{\prime}
(Steps (ii) and (iii-a))

(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
)
.
The bottom-up approach
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)

is given in Figure
1(c)
.
Here no global type is used;
instead, we first infer
the
minimum local type

{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}

from each process
P_{i}

by the
type inference system
(Step (i)).
The minimum type
{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}
of
P
characterises, in a succinct way,
the tightest behaviour of
P
.
In Step (ii),
we directly check whether a property
\varphi
is satisfied or not
against a collection of the minimum types. This guarantees
a set of processes satisfy property
\varphi
.

Road map.

For Step (i) of the top-down, we consider four kinds of projections,
and give analyses of their complexity:

inductive plain merge
, introduced in the first MPST paper

(
Honda et al., 2008
;
Honda et al., 2016
)

(Definition
4.6
,
\mathsection
4.2.1
),

inductive full merge
, refined from plain merge
to obtain a larger set of
valid global types (Definition
4.7
,
\mathsection
4.2.2
),
a recent projection algorithm proposed
by Tirore
et al.

(
Tirore et al., 2023
)
, called
tbc
, which is
sound and complete against the coinductive
projection (
\mathsection
4.3
)
and a new projection algorithm which is sound and complete
against the coinductive projection with full merge
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)

(
\mathsection
4.4
).
For Step (ii), we develop a subtyping system
based on a graph representation of types (called
type graphs
),
which is more efficient than the existing inductive algorithm
in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
(
\mathsection
3
).
This type graph representation turns out
useful for building algorithms for other complexity analyses.

Step (iii-a) of the top-down is type-checking each process by
some subtype
{\sf T}_{i}^{\prime}
of its projection
{\sf T}_{i}
.
This problem is reducible to
subtype-checking
with the minimum type
{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}_{i}
inferred from the process
(
{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}_{i}\leqslant{\sf T}_{i}
) since the projected type
{\sf T}_{i}
must be a
supertype of
{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}_{i}
.
Hence the total complexity of the top-down
is analysed as described in Figure
1(b)
.

In
\mathsection
5
,
we give a sound and complete algorithm for finding a minimum type
by building
type constraints
(Definition
5.4
)
and a
minimum type graph
(Definition

5.15
) which yields the minimum type
of a given process. We then analyse its complexity.
This corresponds to Step (ii-b) of the top-down
(Figure
1(b)
)
and Step (i) in the bottom-up (Figure
1(c)
).
In
\mathsection
6
, we analyse the complexity to check
whether a set of local types satisfy

safety
(no type nor communication error),

deadlock-freedom
(no stuck)
and
liveness
(all pending actions can always
eventually communicate with their dual actions under fair paths).
In
\mathsection
7
we measure
the total of complexities of the top-down
(Steps (i,iii,ii-b) in Figure
1(b)
)
and the bottom-up
(Steps (i,ii) in Figure
1(c)
).

To examine the complexity analyses
covering a wide range of the literature of session types,
we focus on the simplest calculus from
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
, namely
a
synchronous single multiparty session without type
annotations
.
This choice is made because (1) the bottom-up approach is

undecidable
if the calculus is asynchronous (communications use
infinite FIFO queues); and
(2) most of the binary and multiparty session calculi studied in the literature
and a textbook
(
Gay and Vasconcelos, 2025
)
are synchronous (both in theory and practice).
See
\mathsection

7
for more discussions on synchronous and asynchronous MPST.

Contributions.

To our best knowledge, all the complexity results proven in this
paper were unknown.

To calculate the total complexity of the top-down and the bottom-up
approaches, we build the three new systems currently missing from the
workflows:
(1)
a type inference system for MPST
(
\mathsection
5
);
(2)
a quadratic subtyping
algorithm (
\mathsection
3
): and
(3)
a sound and
complete
PSPACE
-algorithm for a coinductive projection with full
merging (
\mathsection
4.4
).

Throughout this paper, we use different formats of
local and
global type graphs
to construct the new systems and measure the
complexity.
Specifically, the type inference system (1)
uses a new form of type graphs (Definition
5.15
) and a relationship between them (Lemma
5.19
)
for finding the minimum
type
{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}
of
P
.
The algorithm generates the
minimum
type graph
, taking care of contravariance of
branching (external choice) and covariance of selections (internal
choice).

The subtyping system (2) (Definition
3.8
) uses
a product graph of two local type graphs
ensuring more efficiency (quadratic) (Theorem
3.13
)
than the existing (exponential) inductive algorithm

(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
(Theorem
3.11
).
(3) is constructed by a
projection graph
of
{\sf G}
whose node is
a set of subglobal types of
{\sf G}
.
Apart from (1)-(3) listed above, we also use
a product of global and local type graphs for complexity analysis
of a projection algorithm by Tirore
et al.

(
Tirore et al., 2023
)
(Theorem
4.22
).

For the complexity analysis of the projection algorithm with

inductive full merging
, if we use
a naïve approach,
the complexity stays as quadratic as

inductive plain merging
(Theorem
4.9
).
This is improved
to subquadratic time (Theorem
4.10
) by
applying the
balanced binary tree search technique
to
the choices (branching and selection) in the session types.
The proof requires a detailed analysis.

Another main contribution is the complexity analysis of the
type context property-checking which was (as far as we have known)
never tackled before our work (Figure
1(c)
(ii)).
One of the most non-trivial proofs in the paper is
PSPACE
-completeness
for the property checking.

For
PSPACE
-membership (
\mathsection
6.2
),
safety and deadlock-freedom are proven as a reachability problem
of checking unsafe and deadlock states, respectively.
For liveness,
we give the equivalent characterisation
by constructing the
counterwitness for liveness
using
observational and barbed predicates on the typing contexts.
With this, we construct a nondeterministic polynomial-space algorithm that aspects if and only if the input action is not live.
We further give deterministic algorithms for safety, deadlock-freedom and
liveness that run in exponential time.
For liveness, we use a similar construction to the proof of Savitch’s theorem

(
Sipser, 2013
, Theorem 8.5)
.

For
PSPACE
-hardness (
\mathsection
6.3
),
we reduce it from the
quantified Boolean formula (QBF) problem
.
We construct a very large typing context that reaches an undesirable state if
a quantified boolean formula is true.
The complexity analysis of
the liveness checking is most involved,
resulting the liveness incurring the highest complexity among the three properties.

Our proof methods for
PSPACE
-completeness are uniformly applicable
to analyse other properties such as termination and other forms of liveness
studied in
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
.

The detailed complexity results of the subtyping checking,
type-level and total complexity of the top-down
and the bottom-up approaches are summarised in
Table
1
(subtyping),
Table
2
(top-down)
and Table
3
(bottom-up).

Appendices include detailed full proofs, omitted algorithms, examples and definitions.

2.
Multiparty Session Calculus

This section introduces the syntax and semantics of a
multiparty synchronous session calculus (MPST-calculus)

(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
and define its properties.

Syntax

Expressions
(
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{e}^{\prime},\dots
) are variables (
x,y,z,\dots
),
a value
\mathsf{v}
, which is either a natural number
\mathsf{n}
, an integer

\mathsf{i}
,
or a boolean
\mathsf{true}
/
\mathsf{false}
,
or a term built from expressions by applying operators such as

{\tt neg}
,
\neg
,
\oplus
or
+
.
The operator
\oplus
models non-determinism:
\mathsf{e}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{e}_{2}
is an expression that might yield either
\mathsf{e}_{1}
or
\mathsf{e}_{2}
.

\mathsf{e}::=\mathsf{true}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathsf{false}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathsf{n}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathsf{i}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~x~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathsf{e}\vee\mathsf{e}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\neg\mathsf{e}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathsf{e}+\mathsf{e}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathsf{e}\oplus\mathsf{e}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{\tt neg}(\mathsf{e})

Processes
(
P,Q,\dots
) and
multiparty sessions
(
M,M^{\prime},\dots
) are defined by the following syntax:

\begin{array}[]{rll}P::=&\mathbf{0}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).P~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleleft l.{P}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}&\text{\footnotesize(nil, output, input, selection, branch)}\\
~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}&\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P~\mathsf{else}~P~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mu X.P~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~X&\text{\footnotesize(conditional, recursion, proc variable)}\\
M::=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,P~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~M~|~M&\text{\footnotesize(role, parallel composition)}\end{array}

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}},\dots
are
participants
from a countably infinite set. The nil
\mathbf{0}
denotes termination;
the output process
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.Q
sends the value of expression
\mathsf{e}

to participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
; the input process
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).P

inputs a value from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
;
the selection
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleleft l.{P}
selects a label
l
at participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
(internal choice); the branching

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}

(often denoted by
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:P_{1},\dots,l_{n}:P_{n}\}
)
is a process that can accept label
l_{i}
from participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
for any
i\in I\neq\emptyset
(external choice).
Conditional process
\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P~\mathsf{else}~Q

and a recursive process
\mu X.P
are standard.
We assume that the recursive processes are
guarded
, i.e.,

\mu X.X
is not a valid process, but

\mu X.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.X
is valid.
Process
P
plays the role of participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
(denoted:
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,P
),
and can interact with other processes playing other roles.
We assume
~|~
is commutative and associative,
and write
\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}\,\mathbf{::}\,P_{i}
for

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{1}\,\mathbf{::}\,P_{1}~|~\cdots~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{n}\,\mathbf{::}\,P_{n}
with
I=\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Reductions

The value
\mathsf{v}
of expression
\mathsf{e}
(notation
\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{v}
) is
computed as expected
(Table
4
in
Appendix
A
).The internal choice
\mathsf{e}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{e}_{2}
evaluates either to the value of
\mathsf{e}_{1}
or to the value of
\mathsf{e}_{2}
. The
reduction rules of multiparty sessions
are given in Figure
2
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{@{}rll}\text{{\small[{r-comm}]}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}?(x).P\ |\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.Q\;~|~\;M\longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,P[\mathsf{v}/x]\;~|~\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,Q\;~|~\;M&(\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{v})\\
\text{{\small[{r-bra}]}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l.{P}~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\;~|~\;M\longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,P~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,P_{k}\;~|~\;M&(k\in I)\\
\text{{\small[{t-cond}]}}&{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P~\mathsf{else}~Q\;~|~\;M\longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,P\;~|~\;M}&(\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{true})\\
\text{{\small[{f-cond}]}}&{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P~\mathsf{else}~Q\;~|~\;M\longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,Q\;~|~\;M}&(\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{false})\\
\text{{\small[{r-str}]}}&M^{\prime}_{1}\Rrightarrow M_{1}\hskip 9.24994ptM_{1}\longrightarrow M_{2}\hskip 9.24994ptM_{2}\Rrightarrow M^{\prime}_{2}\ \Longrightarrow\ M^{\prime}_{1}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}_{2}\\
\text{{\small[{v-err}]}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P~\mathsf{else}~Q\;~|~\;M\longrightarrow\mathsf{error}&(\mathsf{e}\downarrow\mathsf{v}\text{ and }\mathsf{v}\not\in\{\mathsf{true},\mathsf{false}\})\\
\text{{\small[{c-err}]}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l.{P}~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\;~|~\;M\longrightarrow\mathsf{error}&(\forall i\in I,l\not=l_{k})\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 2
.
Reductions of sessions

Rule
[
t-comm
]
defines a communication between
the input and output processes; in
[
t-bra
]
, the selection
process chooses label
l
at the branching;
rules
[
t,f-cond
]
are standard; and
rule
[
t-str
]
defines that a reduction is
closed under
structure rules

\Rrightarrow
(preorder) defined by

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,\mu X.P~|~M\Rrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,P[\mu X.P/X]~|~M
,
and
\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}\,\mathbf{::}\,P_{i}\Rrightarrow\prod_{j\in J}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{j}\,\mathbf{::}\,P_{j}
where
I
is a permutation of
J
.
Rules
[
v-err
]
and
[
s-err
]
define
value and session errors, respectively.
We use
\longrightarrow^{\ast}
as the reflexive and transitive closure of
\longrightarrow
.

Properties

We recall various desirable properties of a session
M

from
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
, Definition 5.1)
.

Definition 2.1 (Properties).

A session
M
is:

(1)

communication safe
iff

M\longrightarrow^{*}M^{\prime}
, then
M^{\prime}\not\Rrightarrow\mathsf{error}
.

(2)

deadlock-free
iff
M\longrightarrow^{*}M^{\prime}\not\longrightarrow
implies

M^{\prime}\Rrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}::\mathbf{0}
.

(3)

live
iff
M\longrightarrow^{*}M^{\prime}\Rrightarrow M_{1}~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}::P
implies:

(a)

If
P={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}?(x).P^{\prime}
then there exists
M_{2},\mathsf{v}
such that
M^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}M_{2}~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}::P^{\prime}[\mathsf{v}/x]
.

(b)

If
P={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P^{\prime}
then there exists
M_{2}
such that
M^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}M_{2}~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}::P^{\prime}
.

(c)

If
P={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l.P^{\prime}
then there exists
M_{2}
such that
M^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}M_{2}~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}::P^{\prime}
.

(d)

If
P={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
then there exists
M_{2},i
such that
M^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}M_{2}~|~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}::P_{i}
.

A session
M
is

communication safe

if there is neither type and label errors;

deadlock-free
when it only stops
reducing to nil;

M
is
live
when all
the pending actions can always
eventually communicate.

3.
Complexity Analysis of Multiparty Session Subtyping

We start from complexity analysis of the two subtyping algorithms,
introducing
type graphs
.
The first algorithm is the inductive algorithm of
multiparty synchronous session subtyping given in

(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
; the second
one is a new coinductive algorithm for multiparty, extending
the algorithm for binary session types in
(
Udomsrirungruang and Yoshida, 2024
)
.

Local Multiparty Session Types
(
{\sf T},{\sf T}^{\prime},\dots
) give a
local specification
of each participant, which are defined with
sorts
(base types) (
S,S^{\prime},\dots
).

\begin{array}[]{c}S::=\>\mathtt{bool}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathtt{nat}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathtt{int}\qquad{\sf T}::=\mathtt{end}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathbf{t}\end{array}

Sorts include base (atomic) types; in general, the choice
of sorts is unimportant and our system will work with arbitrary atomic
sorts.
Type
\mathtt{end}
represents a termination;
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}
is the
output

to participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
with
its value typed by
S
;
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}
is its dual
input
;

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
(with
I\neq\emptyset
)
is a
selection type
which selects one of the labels
l_{i}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
(with
I\neq\emptyset
) is
a
branching type
which offers branches with label
l_{i}
.
We assume all labels are distinct, and often write

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1},\dots,l_{n}:{\sf T}_{n}\}
or

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1},\dots,l_{n}:{\sf T}_{n}\}
.

\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}
and
\mathbf{t}
are recursive types and type variables.
We often omit
\mathtt{end}
and write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1}

for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1}\}
.
We assume a countably infinite set of recursive type variables and,
as with processes, all recursive types to be guarded.
We denote
\mathtt{ftv}({\sf T})
as a set of
free type variables
in
{\sf T}
,
and call
{\sf T}

closed
if
\mathtt{ftv}({\sf T})=\emptyset
.
We denote
\mathcal{S}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}
) as the set of all sorts
(resp. closed local types).

We calculate the complexity against the size of a local type;
and use the set of
subformulas
of
{\sf T}
.

Definition 3.1 (Size of Local Types).

The
size
of a local type
{\sf T}
, denoted
|{\sf T}|
, is defined as:

|\mathtt{end}|=|\mathbf{t}|=1
,

|\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}|=|{\sf T}|+1
,

|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}|=|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}|=|{\sf T}|+1
and

|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}|=|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}|=\sum_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|+1
.

Definition 3.2 (Subformulas of Local Types).

The set of
subformulas

of a local type
{\sf T}
, denoted
{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T})
, is defined as:

{\rm{Sub}}(\mathtt{end})=\{\mathtt{end}\}
,

{\rm{Sub}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T})=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}\}\cup{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T})
,

{\rm{Sub}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T})=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}\}\cup{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T})
,

{\rm{Sub}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I})=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T}_{i})
,

{\rm{Sub}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I})=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T}_{i})
,

{\rm{Sub}}(\mathbf{t})=\{\mathbf{t}\}
and

{\rm{Sub}}(\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T})=\{\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}\}\cup\{{\sf T}^{\prime}[\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}/\mathbf{t}]\mid{\sf T}^{\prime}\in{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T})\}
.

Subtyping.

We use the (coinductive) synchronous multiparty subtyping relation
(denoted by
{\sf T}\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
) following

(
Demangeon and Honda, 2011
;
Ghilezan et al., 2019
;
Chen et al., 2017
)

(called
process subtyping

in
(
Gay, 2016
)
):
when a type
{\sf T}
is “smaller” than
{\sf T}^{\prime}
, it is allowed to use a process typed by the former whenever a process typed by the latter is required.
The original subtyping in
(
Gay and Hole, 2005
)

is called
channel subtyping
in
(
Gay, 2016
)
, and
the complexity results in this paper do not change
whether it is process- or channel-subtyping.

We use the following unfolding function from
(
Gay and Hole, 2005
)
.

Definition 3.3 (Unfolding function).

Define the
unfold
function by:
{\mathsf{unfold}}({\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}})={\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}[\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}/\mathbf{t}]})
, and
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})={\sf T}
otherwise.

Example 3.4 (Unfolding).

Let
{\sf T}_{1}=\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}\},l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}
.
Then we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})=&{\mathsf{unfold}}({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}\},l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}[{\sf T}_{1}/\mathbf{t}]})={\mathsf{unfold}}({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1}\},l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}})\\
=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1}\},l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}\end{array}

We recall the standard definition of coinductive synchronous subtyping
from
(
Gay and Hole, 2005
)
.

Definition 3.5 (Subtyping).

The
subtyping relation

\leqslant
is the largest relation between session types such that, for all
{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}
:

•

If
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})=\mathtt{end}
then
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{2}})=\mathtt{end}
.

•

If
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}}
then
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{2}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}}
and
{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}
.

•

If
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}}
then
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{2}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}}
and
{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}
.

•

If
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{1i}\}_{i\in I}
then
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{2}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{j}:{\sf T}_{2j}\}_{j\in J}
and
I\subseteq J
and
\forall i\in I.\>{\sf T}_{1i}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2i}
.

•

If
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{1i}\}_{i\in I}
then
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{2}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{j}:{\sf T}_{2j}\}_{j\in J}
and
J\subseteq I
and
\forall j\in J.\>{\sf T}_{1j}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2j}
.

Notice that the selection subtyping is covariant and
the branching subtyping is contravariant
with respect to the indices of choices.
The relation
\leqslant
coincides with the coinductive
synchronous subtyping formulation in

(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
, Definition 3.15)
.

We represent types syntactically as labelled transition systems
where each node is a subformula of the type. Type graphs are
used throughout the paper.

Definition 3.6 (Type graph).

Define
actions
:
{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}^{\prime},\dots::=\ {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathsf{end}}
and the
labelled transition relation
(LTS),
{\sf T}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}^{\prime}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}
is an action and
{\sf T}^{\prime}
is extended with the additional
type
\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}
:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 15.35765pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{${\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})=\mathtt{end}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{${\sf T}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\hskip 18.49988pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 40.31844pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{${\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\dagger\in\{!,?\}$}}\hbox{${\sf T}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle}{\sf T}^{\prime}$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\hskip 18.49988pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 69.22949pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{${\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})={{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\dagger\{{l_{i}:{{\sf T}_{i}}}\}_{i\in I}\hskip 9.24994pt\dagger\in\{\oplus,\&\}\hskip 9.24994ptj\in I$}}\hbox{${\sf T}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\dagger{l_{j}}}{{\sf T}_{j}}$}}}\par}\par}}}$}
}\end{array}

The type graph is a directed graph whose edges are local types or

\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}
, and an edge is given by
{\sf T}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}^{\prime}

(from
{\sf T}
to
{\sf T}^{\prime}
labelled by
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}
).
The type graph for
{\sf T}
, denoted by
\mathbb{G}({\sf T})
,
is the graph reachable by the transitions from
{\sf T}
.
We often write
{\sf T}
for
\mathbb{G}({\sf T})
when it is clear from the context.

Example 3.7 (Transitions).

Recall
{\sf T}_{1}
in Example
3.4
.
Then we have:

{\sf T}_{1}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{1}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1}\}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{1}}}{\sf T}_{1}
and

{\sf T}_{1}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{2}}}\mathtt{end}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}
.
Its type graph
\mathbb{G}({\sf T})
is in Figure
3(a)
.

{\sf T}_{1}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1}\}
\mathtt{end}
\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{1}}

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{1}}
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{2}}
\mathsf{end}

(a)
\mathbb{G}({\sf T})
for Example
3.7
.

({\sf T}_{1},{\sf T}_{2})
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{\sf T}_{1}\},{\sf T}_{2})
(\mathtt{end},\mathtt{end})
(\mathsf{Skip},\mathsf{Skip})

(b)

{\sf T}_{1}\lesssim{\sf T}_{2}
for Example
3.10
.

Figure 3
.
Local type graphs

We redefine the subtyping relation equivalently in terms of a
simulation

between the type graphs.

Definition 3.8 (Type simulations).

A
type simulation

\mathcal{R}
is a relation on
type graphs
{\sf T}_{1}
and
{\sf T}_{2}
such that

{\sf T}_{1}\mathcal{R}{\sf T}_{2}
implies:

•

If
{\sf T}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}
then
{\sf T}_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}
,
and
{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{R}{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}
, for

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}\in\{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l},\mathtt{end},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle\}
.

•

If
{\sf T}_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}
then
{\sf T}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}
,
and
{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{R}{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}
, for

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}\in\{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l},\mathtt{end}\}
.

The subtyping simulation
\lesssim
is defined by
{\sf T}_{1}\lesssim{\sf T}_{2}

if
({\sf T}_{1},{\sf T}_{2})\in\mathcal{R}

in some type simulation
\mathcal{R}
, i.e. subtyping is the largest type
simulation.
We write
\simeq

for the type graph equivalence.

Lemma 3.9 (Subtyping as a type
simulation).

(
Proof in
Appendix
B
)

{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}
iff
{\sf T}_{1}\lesssim{\sf T}_{2}
.

Example 3.10 (Type simulation).

Recall
{\sf T}_{1}=\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}\},l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}

from Example
3.7

and
{\sf T}_{2}=\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t},l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}
.

{\sf T}_{1}
is a subtype of
{\sf T}_{2}
; the graph for the type simulation is in Figure
3(b)
.

Ghilezan
et al.
give an inductive subtype-checking
algorithm
defined
in Figure
10
(extending the one
for binary in
(
Gay and Hole, 2005
)
). The rules take
the judgement of the form
\Theta\vdash{\sf T}\leq{\sf T}^{\prime}
, which means “assuming the relations in
\Theta
, we may conclude that
{\sf T}
is a subtype of
{\sf T}^{\prime}
”.
They defined, but did not analyse its complexity.
We prove the following result that
their algorithm takes
exponential
time
.

Theorem 3.11.

Let
n=|{\sf T}_{1}|+|{\sf T}_{2}|
.
The upper bound of the worst-case complexity of
the algorithm to check

{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}

given in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
, Table 6)
is
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(n^{n^{3}}\right)
,
and the lower bound of the worst-case complexity
is
\Omega\negmedspace\left((\sqrt{n})!\right)
.

Subtyping checking can be performed
in quadratic time using the simulation
\lesssim
.
For fixed types
{\sf T}_{1}
and
{\sf T}_{2}
, in a type derivation tree, one may need to prove
\Gamma\vdash{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}

multiple times (possibly with different values of
\Gamma
), whereas using a subtyping
graph all of these judgements are combined into a single node.

To describe the subtyping algorithm,
we use the following definition, called

inconsistent nodes
.
In
\mathbb{G}({\sf T},{\sf T}^{\prime})
, a state
({\sf T}_{1}^{\prime},{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime})
is
inconsistent

if it immediately violates Definition
3.8
.

Definition 3.12 (Inconsistent nodes).

A state
({\sf T}_{1}^{\prime},{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime})
in

\mathbb{G}({\sf T},{\sf T}^{\prime})

is
inconsistent
iff at least one of the following hold, for some
{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
:

•

{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}\not\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}\in\{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l},\mathtt{end},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle\}
.

•

{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and
{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}\not\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}\in\{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l},\mathtt{end}\}
.

Theorem 3.13.

(
Proof in
Appendix
B.2
)
There exists an algorithm for checking
{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}

with worst-case time complexity

\Theta\negmedspace\left(|{\sf T}_{1}|\cdot|{\sf T}_{2}|\right)
.

Proof.

(upper bound)

For the algorithm for checking
{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}
,
we first construct a subtyping graph
\mathbb{G}({\sf T}_{1},{\sf T}_{2})
.
Then for each node
({\sf T}_{1}^{\prime},{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime})
in the graph,
check if it is inconsistent. Next we add
the edge
({\sf T}_{1},{\sf T}_{2})\rightarrow({\sf T}_{1}^{\prime},{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime})
if
{\sf T}_{1}\,\mathcal{R}\,{\sf T}_{2}

directly implies
{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathcal{R}\,{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}
in the definition of
\lesssim
.
If there is any reachable inconsistent node,
then
{\sf T}_{1}\not\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}
; otherwise,
{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}
.
To show correctness of the algorithm,
observe that any set of consistent nodes closed
under reachability is a type simulation,
directly from Definition
3.8
.
The minimal such set containing
({\sf T}_{1},{\sf T}_{2})
,
if it exists, must be the set of reachable nodes from
({\sf T}_{1},{\sf T}_{2})
.
Thus we can check if any inconsistent nodes are contained in this set to solve the problem.
Since the graph has
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|{\sf T}_{1}|\cdot|{\sf T}_{2}|\right)
nodes and edges,
the algorithm runs in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|{\sf T}_{1}|\cdot|{\sf T}_{2}|\right)
time with a reachability search.

(lower bound)

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, consider

{\sf T}_{i}=\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle;\dots{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle;\mathbf{t}
where

{|{\sf T}_{i}|}=n_{i}+2
(i.e., there are
n_{i}
-times
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle

under the
recursive body).
The type graph of
{\sf T}_{i}
is isomorphic to the graph on
n_{i}
vertices
\{0,\dots,n_{i}-1\}
, and
j\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle}((j+1)\bmod n_{i})
.
Thus the subtyping graph has transitions
(j,k)\rightarrow((j+1)\bmod n_{1},(k+1)\bmod n_{2})
with starting node
(0,0)
.
If
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
are coprime then all
n_{1}\cdot n_{2}
nodes are reachable.
We have that
{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}
thus the algorithm must search the entire graph,
and therefore the complexity in this case is
\Theta\negmedspace\left(|{\sf T}_{1}|\cdot|{\sf T}_{2}|\right)
.
∎

A summary of the results in this section is in Table
1
.

4.
Complexity Analyses of Four End-Point Projections

This section studies complexity of the four existing
end-point projections. More precisely,
for the
inductive plain and full merging
, as well as the
coinductive plain merging

projections, we give the upper and lower bounds of
the worst-case complexity of the projection algorithms.
For the
coinductive full merging
projection,
we define a new sound and complete
projection algorithm for coinductive projection with full merging using type graphs (Definition
4.27
).
We then give the upper and lower bounds of
the worst-case complexity of

the projection problem
(which asks the projection of given
{\sf G}
) for
the coinductive full merging projection.
Figure
4
depicts the relationship
between sets of local types produced by
the four projections.
The table in the right hand side (1-4) lists examples
which represent different projections.
They are explained throughout this section.
Liveness, deadlock-freedom and safety properties (5-9) are explained in

\mathsection
6.1
.

6.3
)

6.6
)

4

6.8
)

,

)

,

)

,

)

,

)

Example

1

{\sf G}_{\text{ip}}

Example
