Figure 4.
Projections and typing context properties.
The numbers correspond to examples in the right table
which separate properties.
For example, the typing context
\Delta_{9}
in
4
is
safe but not deadlock-free.

4.1.
Global Types

We define
global types
, which give a global view
of interactions between all the participants. Global types are
projected to a set of local types by the algorithms defined in the next subsection.

Definition 4.1 (Global type).

Global types

{\sf G},{\sf G}^{\prime},{\sf G}_{i},\dots

are defined by the following syntax:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\sf G}::=\mathtt{end}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>{\sf G}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\mathbf{t}\end{array}

The
message type
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>{\sf G}
, denotes
a message from participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
to participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}
with a payload of type

S
; the
branching type
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
defines that participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
can select label
l_{i}
at participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}
where
I\not=\emptyset
and
l_{i}\not=l_{j}
with
i\not=j
for all
i,j\in I
;

\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G}
is a
recursive type
(where we assume
{\sf G}
is guarded);
and
\mathbf{t}
is a
type variable
.
The set of participants of a global type
{\sf G}
is defined as:

{\mathtt{pt}}\{\mathbf{t}\}={\mathtt{pt}}\{\mathtt{end}\}=\emptyset
;

{\mathtt{pt}}\{\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G}\}={\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}
;

{\mathtt{pt}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>{\sf G}\}={\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}\cup\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\}
; and

{\mathtt{pt}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\}=\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}{{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}_{i}\}}\right)\cup\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\}
.
We denote
\mathtt{ftv}({\sf G})
as the set of
free type variables
in
{\sf G}

and if
\mathtt{ftv}({\sf G})=\emptyset
, we call
{\sf G}

closed
.

We define the size
|{\sf G}|
and subformulas of global types
{\rm{Sub}}({\sf G})
analogously to local types.

Definition 4.2 (Size of a global type).

The size of a global type
{\sf G}
, denoted
|{\sf G}|
, is defined as:

|\mathbf{t}|=|\mathtt{end}|=1
;
|\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G}|=1+|{\sf G}|
;
|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>{\sf G}|=1+|{\sf G}|
; and
|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}|=1+\sum_{i\in I}|{\sf G}_{i}|
.

Definition 4.3 (Subformulas of global types).

The set of
subformulas of a global type
{\sf G}
, denoted
{\rm{Sub}}({\sf G})
, is defined as:

{\rm{Sub}}(\mathtt{end})=\mathtt{end}
;
{\rm{Sub}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>{\sf G})=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>{\sf G}\}\cup{\rm{Sub}}({\sf G})
;

{\rm{Sub}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I})=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}{\rm{Sub}}({\sf G}_{i})
; and

{\rm{Sub}}(\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G})=\{{\sf G}\}\cup\{{\sf G}^{\prime}[\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G}/\mathbf{t}]\mid{\sf G}^{\prime}\in{\rm{Sub}}({\sf G})\}
.

Definition 4.4 (Unfolding of a global type).

We define
{\mathsf{unfold}}({\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G}})={\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}[\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G}/\mathbf{t}]})
, and
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={\sf G}
otherwise.

4.2.
Two Inductive Projections

Firstly, we consider the two algorithms of
inductive projections
,
which are defined purely syntactically.
This comes in two variants:
plain merging

from
(
Honda et al., 2008
;
Honda et al., 2016
)
which merges only
identical types if the participant is not involved.
The second one, called
full merging
from
(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
)
,
gives more projectable global types,
enabling merging of two different branchings.

Definition 4.5 (Inductive projection).

The projection operator on participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
for global type
{\sf G}
,
denoted by
{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
, is a partial function defined as:

•

{\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf G}\right)}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}=\begin{cases}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}&\text{if }{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}&\text{if }{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\\
{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}&\hskip-5.69054pt\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

{\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\right)}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}=\begin{cases}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{{\sf G}_{i}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\}_{i\in I}&\text{if }{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{{\sf G}_{i}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\}_{i\in I}&\text{if }{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\\
{{{\sqcap}}}_{i\in I}{{\sf G}_{i}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

•

{\left(\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G}\right)}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}=\begin{cases}\mathtt{end}&\text{if }{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\not\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}\text{ and }\mathtt{ftv}(\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf G})=\emptyset\\
\mu\mathbf{t}.\left({{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\right)&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

{\mathtt{end}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}=\mathtt{end}

where the merging operator
\sqcap
is defined in Definitions
4.6
and
4.7
below.
If undefined by the above rules, then
{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
is undefined.

Definition 4.6 (Inductive plain merging).

The plain merge is defined by
{\sf T}\sqcap{\sf T}={\sf T}
, and undefined otherwise.

Definition 4.7 (Inductive full merging).

The full merge is defined inductively by:

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{1}}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.08331pt\hskip-3.08331pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.08331pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.33682pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.08331pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{2}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle;{(}{\sf T}_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.08331pt\hskip-3.08331pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.08331pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.33682pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.08331pt}}{\sf T}_{2})
with
\dagger\in\{!,?\}
;

\mathbf{t}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.08331pt\hskip-3.08331pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.08331pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.33682pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.08331pt}}\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{t}
;

\mathtt{end}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.08331pt\hskip-3.08331pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.08331pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.33682pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.08331pt}}\mathtt{end}=\mathtt{end}
;

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.08331pt\hskip-3.08331pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.08331pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.33682pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.08331pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.08331pt\hskip-3.08331pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.08331pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.33682pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.08331pt}}{\sf T}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
; and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.08331pt\hskip-3.08331pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.08331pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.33682pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.08331pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{j}:{\sf T}_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j\in J}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\left(\{l_{k}:({\sf T}_{k}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.08331pt\hskip-3.08331pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.08331pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.33682pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.08331pt}}{\sf T}_{k}^{\prime})\mid k\in I\cap J\}\cup\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\mid i\in I\setminus J\}\cup\{l_{j}:{\sf T}_{j}^{\prime}\mid j\in J\setminus I\}\right)
;

and undefined otherwise.

Note that we can show that both notions of merging are associative and commutative
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
.
We introduce the notation:
{{{\sqcap}}}_{i\in I}{\sf T}_{i}=(\dots({\sf T}_{n_{1}}\sqcap{\sf T}_{n_{2}})\dots)\sqcap{\sf T}_{n_{m}}
for finite non-empty set
I=\{n_{1},\dots,n_{m}\}
, and similarly for
{{{\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 5.76001pt\hskip-5.76001pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-5.76001pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{3.63683pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 5.76001pt}}}}}
.

Notice that in full merging (Definition
4.6
), while the internal choice does not increase
the branchings, the two external choices can be merged to make
a larger type with more branchings.
This is consistent with subtyping: given some types
with external choices, merging the choices together keeps them as
subtypes of the original types, and such can be used in place of
them. For internal choice, adding more choices makes
an ill-behaved global type well-formed. See
(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
, Example
3.13)
and
(
Honda et al., 2016
,
\mathsection
4.3)
.

The following example illustrates that full merging is more expressive than plain merging.

Example 4.8.

Consider two global types where
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}
is sending
a label to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
after receiving from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\sf G}_{\text{ip}}\ =&\mu\mathbf{t}.\>{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}\},l_{2}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}}}\}\}\\
{\sf G}_{\text{if}}\ =&\mu\mathbf{t}.\>{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}\},l_{2}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{2}:\mathtt{end}}}\}\}\end{array}

Then we have

{{\sf G}_{\text{ip}}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\ =\ \mu\mathbf{t}.\>{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}\}
by both plain and full merging,
but:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{{\sf G}_{\text{if}}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\ =&\mu\mathbf{t}.\>({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}\}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\sqcap\>{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}})=\mu\mathbf{t}.\>({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t}\}\sqcap{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\&\!\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\})\end{array}

By plain merging, this is
undefined
; by full merging, it is
defined
as:
\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t},l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}
.

4.2.1.
Complexity of the projection with plain merging

We show that inductive projection of a global type
{\sf G}
with plain merging is in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|{\sf G}|\log|{\sf G}|\right)
time.
Most cases of inductive projection can be performed in constant time and reduce the total sizes of the types to be projected by a constant amount; the only exception is the projection of
{\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\right)}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
where
|I|\geq 2
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\notin\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\}
.
In this case the syntactic equality check takes linear time and the size of each projected branch is at most half of the size of the original type. Theorem
4.9
states this argument.

Theorem 4.9.

(
Proof in
Appendix
C.1
)
There exists an algorithm for inductive projection with plain merging
with worst-case time complexity
\Theta\negmedspace\left({|{\sf G}|}\log{|{\sf G}|}\right)
.

Proof.

(upper bound)

First, checking

\mathtt{ftv}({\sf G})=\emptyset

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\notin{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}

for every syntax of subformulas of
{\sf G}

is performed in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(n\right)
time by

{|{\rm{Sub}}({\sf G})|}=\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|{\sf G}|\right)
.
We also note that

{|{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}|}\leq{|{\sf G}|}
.
Then, our algorithm performs the projection in the same recursive sense as in Definition
4.5
, except for the aforementioned checking of
\mathtt{ftv}({\sf G})=\emptyset
.
First, we prove that the algorithm
computes
{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
in
2|{\sf G}|\log|{\sf G}|
time, if
{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
is defined.
Then, we prove that the time taken when
{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
is undefined is

2|{\sf G}|\log|{\sf G}|+|{\sf G}|
.

(lower bound)

Consider:

{\sf G}_{m}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{1}:{\sf G}_{m-1},l_{2}:{\sf G}_{m-1}\}

(m>0)

and
{\sf G}_{0}=\mathtt{end}
.
We compute
{{\sf G}_{m}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
. Type
{\sf G}_{m}
has size
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(2^{m}\right)
but
computing
{{\sf G}_{k}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\sqcap{{\sf G}_{k}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
requires
\Theta\negmedspace\left(2^{k}\right)
time (because
|{{\sf G}_{k}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}|=\Theta\negmedspace\left(2^{k}\right)
). Thus, the
time taken to compute
{{\sf G}_{m}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
is
\Theta\negmedspace\left(m\cdot 2^{m}\right)
, which
gives rise to the
\Omega\negmedspace\left(n\log n\right)
lower bound.
∎

4.2.2.
Complexity of projection with full merging: Naïve algorithm

In the full merging, as the requirement for merged branches to be
syntactically equal is dropped, unlike the plain merging case, the
branch sizes may be unbalanced.
If we represent type graphs in a
naïve
way (that is, syntactically representing types as finite trees),
the following example introduces a proof that the worst-case runtime of such an algorithm is
\Omega\negmedspace\left(n^{2}\right)
.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}{\sf G}^{(n+1)}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\left\{l_{1}:{\sf G}^{(n)},l_{2}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\left\{l_{n+1}:\mathtt{end}\right\}\right\}&\text{for }n\geq 0\\
{\sf G}^{(0)}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\left\{l_{0}:\mathtt{end}\right\}\end{array}

Theorem 4.10.

Finding
{{\sf G}^{(n)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
under full merging with a naïve representation of types as syntax trees takes
\Theta\negmedspace\left(n^{2}\right)
time.

Proof.

We have
{{\sf G}^{(n)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\mathtt{end}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
.
Therefore, when computing
{{\sf G}^{(n+1)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
from
{{\sf G}^{(n)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
, the naïve algorithm will need to compute
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\mathtt{end}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\>\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}\>{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\&\!\{l_{n+1}:\mathtt{end}\}
. As the size of the types is linear, and merging takes linear time in the naïve algorithm, the
n
-th step uses
\Theta\negmedspace\left(n\right)
time, so the total time is
\Theta\negmedspace\left(n^{2}\right)
.
∎

4.2.3.
Complexity of projection with full merging: Optimised algorithm

We construct the optimised algorithm
using the classical
technique of small-to-large merging: by representing each branch of a
branching type

as a leaf of a binary search tree on their labels, we can efficiently represent
the set of branches of a type, and
merge branching types adding each branch of the smaller type to the
bigger type. Intuitively, each branch is “merged” into a
larger type only
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(\log n\right)
times, but a careful analysis is
needed as merging affects the size of types and is recursive.
Note that our method works for non-binary branching types, and the
nontriviality in the above construction is handling
arbitrary types

with
arbitrarily many branches
in each choice.

Lemma 4.11.

(
Proof in
Appendix
C.2
)

If
\>{\sf T}_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{2}={\sf T}
, then
{\sf T}_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{2}
can be computed in

\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|{\sf T}_{1}|+|{\sf T}_{2}|-|{\sf T}|+|{\sf T}_{2}|\cdot\log|{\sf T}_{1}|\right)
time.

Proof.

By structural induction on
{\sf T}_{1}
.
First note that

{|{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}|}\leq{|{\sf G}|}
under the full merge.
We show that, up to constant
factors, the merge can be computed in time at most
2(|{\sf T}_{1}|+|{\sf T}_{2}|-|{\sf T}|)-1+(|{\sf T}_{2}|-1)\cdot\log|{\sf T}_{1}|
.
Note this is positive by
the fact that
{\sf T}_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{2}={\sf T}
implies
|{\sf T}|<|{\sf T}_{1}|+|{\sf T}_{2}|
(Lemma
C.4
).
To allow for the complexity logarithmic in
|{\sf T}_{1}|
,
we represent branching types as binary search trees.
∎

Theorem 4.12.

(
Proof in
Appendix
C.2
)
There exists an algorithm for inductive projection with full merging
with worst-case time complexity

\Theta\negmedspace\left({|{\sf G}|}\log^{2}{|{\sf G}|}\right)
.

Proof.

(upper bound)

We start by checking every syntactic subterm
{\sf G}
satisfies
\mathtt{ftv}({\sf G})=\emptyset

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\notin{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}
in linear time.
Then, we show inductively that (up to constant factors) we can compute
{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
in time
|{\sf G}|\log^{2}|{\sf G}|+2|{\sf G}|-|{\sf T}|
, where
{\sf T}={{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
.

(lower bound)

Consider the following global types:

{\sf G}_{k}^{(j)}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{1}:{\sf G}_{k-1}^{(2j)},l_{2}:{\sf G}_{k-1}^{(2j+1)}\}
with
k>0
and

{\sf G}_{0}^{(j)}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{j}:\mathtt{end}\}
.
We shall compute
{{\sf G}_{m}^{(0)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\mathtt{end}\}_{i\in\{0,\dots,2^{m}-1\}}
.
We have
{{\sf G}_{k}^{(j)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\mathtt{end}\}_{i\in\{2^{k}\cdot j,\dots,2^{k}\cdot(j+1)-1\}}
and
|{{\sf G}_{k}^{(j)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}|=2^{j}
.
Computing

{{\sf G}_{k-1}^{(2j)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{{\sf G}_{k-1}^{(2j+1)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}

={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\mathtt{end}\}_{i\in\{2^{k-1}\cdot(2j),\dots,2^{k-1}\cdot(2j+1)-1\}}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\mathtt{end}\}_{i\in\{2^{k-1}\cdot(2j+1),\dots,2^{k-1}\cdot(2j+2)-1\}}

requires merging
2^{k-1}
branches into an existing branching type with
2^{k-1}
branches,
thus this requires
2^{k-1}
operations on a balanced binary search tree with
2^{k-1}
leaves.
Hence the merging requires time
\Theta\negmedspace\left(k\cdot 2^{k}\right)
.

We can deduce that the total time required to compute
{{\sf G}_{m}^{(0)}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}
is
\Theta\negmedspace\left(m\cdot 2^{m}\right)
and
|{\sf G}_{m}^{(0)}|=\Theta\negmedspace\left(2^{m}\right)
, which gives rise to the
\Omega\negmedspace\left(n\log^{2}n\right)
lower bound.
∎

4.3.
Tirore
et al.
’s Projection
(
Tirore et al., 2023
)

In the next two subsections, we discuss complexity of
the
coinductive projection
with the plain and full merging
operators.
We define the
coinductive projection
using the unfolding
function following the style in Definition
3.5
.

Definition 4.13 (Coinductive projection).

The
coinductive projection
is the largest relation

{}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}{}
between global and local types such that, when

{{\sf G}}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}{{\sf T}}
:

•

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\notin{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}
implies
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})=\mathtt{end}

•

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf G}^{\prime}
implies

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}^{\prime}
and
{{\sf G}^{\prime}}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}{{\sf T}^{\prime}}
.

•

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf G}^{\prime}
implies
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}^{\prime}
and
{{\sf G}^{\prime}}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}{{\sf T}^{\prime}}
.

•

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf G}^{\prime}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\notin\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\}
implies
{\sf G}^{\prime}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}{{\sf T}}
.

•

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
implies
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}
and
{\sf G}_{i}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}{\sf T}_{i}\ \forall i\in I
.

•

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
implies
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}
and
{\sf G}_{i}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}{\sf T}_{i}\ \forall i\in I
.

•

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
implies
\exists{\sf T}_{i}.\ {\sf T}={{{\sqcap}}}_{i\in I}{\sf T}_{i}
and
{\sf G}_{i}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}}{\sf T}_{i}\ \forall i\in I

for some
merging operator

\sqcap
.

Definition 4.14 (Coinductive plain merging).

Recall
\simeq
denotes the type graph equivalence
(Definition
3.8
).
The
coinductive plain merge
is defined by
{\sf T}\sqcap{\sf T}^{\prime}={\sf T}

if
{\sf T}\simeq{\sf T}^{\prime}
and undefined otherwise.

We first recall
balanced global types
from
Definition 3.3 in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
.

Definition 4.15 (Global type graph).

A
global type graph
of
{\sf G}
(denoted by
\mathbb{G}({\sf G})
) has
nodes
{\rm{Sub}}({\sf G})
and has edges defined by the following rules:

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>{\sf G}^{\prime}
{\sf G}\rightarrow{\sf G}^{\prime}

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
{\sf G}\rightarrow{\sf G}_{i}

Definition 4.16.

A global type
{\sf G}

involves
participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}

if
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf G}^{\prime}

or
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}

with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}\}
. We write
{\mathsf{inv}}({{\sf G}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}})

if
{\sf G}
involves
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
.

Definition 4.17 (Balanced global types, Definition 3.3 in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
).

A global type
{\sf G}
is
balanced
iff, for each node
{\sf G}^{\prime}
in
a global type graph
\mathbb{G}({\sf G})
,
whenever a node involving some
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
is reachable from

{\sf G}^{\prime}
, there exists
k
such that all paths from
{\sf G}^{\prime}
reach a node involving
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
within
k
steps.

The balanced condition is required for
ensuring liveness of session
M
(Definition
2.1
(3))
under the coinductive projection.
For example,
the local types projected from
the global type

{\sf G}^{\prime}=\mu\mathbf{t}.\>{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l:\mathbf{t},\ l^{\prime}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l^{\prime\prime}:\mathtt{end}\}\}

in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
, Example 3.12)

can type
non-live
session
M
.

Lemma 4.18 (Balanced global types).

Suppose
{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
(Definition
4.5
) is defined for

\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}.\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}
.
Then
{\sf G}
is balanced.

Theorem 4.19.

(
Proof in
Appendix
C.4
)
Checking
{\sf G}
balanced can be performed in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|{\sf G}|^{2}\right)
time.

We now give the complexity analysis for the most recent projection
algorithm proposed by Tirore
et al.

(
Tirore et al., 2023
)
, which we call

tbc
.
This algorithm computes a projection on inductive types with plain merge
that is sound and complete with respect to its coinductive projection;
Example
4.20
shows that
syntactic inductive projections may fail to compute a projection when

tbc
may not.
This is because inductive projection considers the syntax of types,
so types with the same infinite tree but different patterns of
\mu
-binders
are not considered equal in merging.
Their algorithm is equivalent to considering types
as infinite trees.

Example 4.20.

Consider the following global type:

{\sf G}_{\text{cp}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{1}:\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>\mathbf{t},l_{2}:\mu\mathbf{t}^{\prime}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;\>\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\}

Both branches of the communication
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}
represent the same tree, but they are not equal syntactically. Therefore, inductive projection (with either kind of merging) will fail to compute a projection. The
tbc
projection is defined as these types represent the same infinite trees. On the other hand,
{{\sf G}_{\text{if}}}
in
Example
4.8
is not projectable
by
tbc
. We shall see
{{\sf G}_{\text{if}}}
is projectable
by coinductive projection
with the full merging defined in Definition
4.23
.

The result of the following lemma is visualised in
Figure
4
.

Proposition 4.21.

(
Proof in
Appendix
C.3
)
If
{\sf G}
is inductively projectable by full merging but
not inductively projectable by plain merging,
then
{\sf G}
is not coinductively projectable by
plain merging.

The
tbc
algorithm is non-trivial and consists of a number of functions
over graph representations of
{\sf G}
and
{\sf T}
,
taking several pages of their paper. Rather than listing
all of the definitions needed for
tbc
, we summarise how we analyse
its complexity. The
tbc
algorithm reduces the problem to one of checking whether a
global type
{\sf G}
is projectable to a local type
{\sf T}
: first, a
candidate projection
\mathsf{trans}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}({\sf G})
is defined, then the

intermediate projection

is checked. It is then shown that this transformation
is sound and complete with respect to a coinductive definition of projection with plain merging.
Specifically, if
{\sf G}
is projectable, it is projectable to a coinductive
type that unravels to
\mathsf{trans}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}({\sf G})

(
Tirore et al., 2023
, Definition 9)
.
In short, two predicates are checked: one for all nodes in a graph representation of
{\sf G}
and another for all nodes in a
product graph
of
{\sf G}
and
{\sf T}
, which has size
|{\sf G}|\cdot|{\sf T}|
.
As
|{\sf T}|\leq|{\sf G}|
,
we arrive at the following theorem.

Theorem 4.22.

(
Proof in
Appendix
C.3
)

The projection in
(
Tirore et al., 2023
)
takes
\Theta\negmedspace\left(|{\sf G}|^{2}\right)
time in the worst case.

4.4.
Coinductive Projection with Full Merging

We define the coinductive projection with
full merging extending from
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
, Definition 3.6)
.

Definition 4.23 (Full coinductive merge).

The
full coinductive merge

\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}
is coinductively
defined by:

•

If
{\sf T}_{1}=\mathtt{end}
and
{\sf T}_{2}=\mathtt{end}
, then
{\sf T}_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{2}=\mathtt{end}
.

•

If
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}}
and
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{2}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}}
,
then
{\sf T}_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{2}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle;{{\sf T}_{1}^{\prime}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{2}^{\prime}}
with
\dagger\in\{!,?\}
.

•

If
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{2}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
,
then
{\sf T}_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{2}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
.

•

If
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{1}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{2}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{j}:{\sf T}_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j\in J}
,

then
{\sf T}_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{2}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{k}:{\sf T}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\}_{k\in I\cup J}
where
(1)
{\sf T}_{k}^{\prime\prime}={\sf T}_{k}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\sqcap$}$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-1.84029pt\raisebox{1.6146pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\text{\tiny$\star$}$}$}}\hskip-1.84029pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\sf T}_{k}^{\prime}
if

k\in I\cap J
;
(2)
{\sf T}_{k}^{\prime\prime}={\sf T}_{k}
if
k\in I\setminus J
;
or (3)
{\sf T}_{k}^{\prime\prime}={\sf T}_{k}^{\prime}
if
k\in J\setminus I
; undefined otherwise.

4.4.1.
Lower bounds for coinductive projection with full merging

We exhibit a global type
{\sf G}
such that the size of the projected
local type graph must be exponential in the syntactic size of

{\sf G}
.

Theorem 4.24.

There exists a (balanced) global type
{\sf G}
whose projection onto some participant requires a local type graph of size
\Omega\negmedspace\left(2^{|{\sf G}|^{c}}\right)
for some
c>0
.

Proof.

We prove this for
c=\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon>0
.
Fix integers
n_{i}
for
i
in some finite indexing set

I
. Consider the following global type:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\sf G}_{\text{cf}}&=\mathrlap{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{i}:\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\Big\{\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$a$}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$a$}:\dots{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$a$}:\mathbf{t}\}\dots\},\ \text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$b$}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{i}:\mathtt{end}\}\Big\}\}_{i\in I}}{\hskip 59.00002pt{\underbrace{\hskip 146.00027pt}_{n_{i}{\text{ times}}}}}\end{array}

Participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
chooses one
i\in I
to follow:
after every multiple of
n_{i}
communications with label
a
to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}
,
it is possible to send a label
b
to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}
.
Thus,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}
must be able to accept
b
precisely after
m

a
messages
if and only if
m
is a multiple of some
n_{i}
.
For coprime
n_{i}
, the sequence of allowable communications is acyclic, so we would require at least
\prod_{i\in I}n_{i}
states of a local type graph.
We have that
|{\sf G}|=1+2\cdot|I|+\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}
.
Taking
n_{i}
as the first
k
primes, as a loose bound we have
|{\sf G}|=\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(k^{2+\varepsilon^{\prime}}\right)
(for all
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
)
by the prime number theorem,
and
\prod_{i\in I}n_{i}=\Omega\negmedspace\left(2^{k}\right)
.
Thus, the required number of states is
\Omega\negmedspace\left(2^{m}\right)
with

m={|{\sf G}|^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\right)}}
.
∎

Type
{\sf G}_{\text{cf}}
also serves as the example which is projecatble
only by the coinductive projection with full merging, but not any of other
three projections.

Corollary 4.25.

The coinductive projection with full merging has worst-case time complexity lower bounded by
\Omega\negmedspace\left(2^{|{\sf G}|^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\right)}}\right)
for some
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

The time complexity of the algorithm is lower-bounded by the size of the output local type graph.
∎

4.4.2.
A
PSPACE
algorithm for coinductive projection with full merging

We give a sound and complete algorithm for coinductive projection with full merging. We first introduce a
subset construction
procedure,
inspired by
(
Li et al., 2024b
)
. We first make a closure
of
{\sf G}
on
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
as a finite set of subglobal types of
{\sf G}

which are reachable without involving
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
.

Definition 4.26 (Closure of a participant).

Let
\mathfrak{g}
denote
a set of closed global types.
For participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
, define the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
-closure

\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\mathfrak{g})
of a set of global types
\mathfrak{g}
as follows:
(1)
{\sf G}\in\mathfrak{g}
implies
{\sf G}\in\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\mathfrak{g})
;
(2)
{\sf G}\in\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\mathfrak{g})
,

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\langle{S^{\prime}}\rangle;{\sf G}^{\prime}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\notin\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\}
imply

{\sf G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\mathfrak{g})
; and
(3)

{\sf G}\in\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\mathfrak{g})
,

{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf G}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}:\{l_{i}:{\sf G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
j\in I

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\notin\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\}
imply

{\sf G}_{j}\in\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Starting from a given global type
{\sf G}
,
the following algorithm produces a local type graph of participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
whose
nodes are a set of global types and edges are
actions from a node to a node. The produced
type graph represents a projection of
{\sf G}
onto
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
.

Definition 4.27 (Subset construction).

Let
{\sf G}
be a closed global type and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}
.
We compute the
projection graph

\mathbb{G}^{\mathsf{proj}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}({\sf G})
of
{\sf G}
onto
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
as the local type graph whose node is a set of subglobal types of
{\sf G}
.
The initial node is set to
\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\{{\sf G}\})
.

[
G-End
]

\forall{\sf G}\in\mathfrak{g}.\ \neg({\mathsf{inv}}({{\sf G}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}))
implies

\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}
.

[
G-Out
]

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{\mathfrak{g}\}
,

{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}
and

{\mathsf{inv}}({{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}})
and

{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf G}_{i}
imply

\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle}\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\{G_{i}\}_{i\in I})
.

[
G-In
]

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{\mathfrak{g}\}
,

{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}
and

{\mathsf{inv}}({{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}})
and

{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf G}_{i}
imply

\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle}\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\{G_{i}\}_{i\in I})
.

[
G-Sel
]

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{\mathfrak{g}\}
,

{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}
and

{\mathsf{inv}}({{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}})
and

{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{j}:{\sf G}_{ij}\}_{j\in J}
imply

\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\oplus{l_{j}}}\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\{{\sf G}_{ij}\}_{j\in J,i\in I})
for all
j\in J
.

[
G-Bra
]

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{\mathfrak{g}\}
and

{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}
and

{\mathsf{inv}}({{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}})
and

{\sf G}_{i}^{\prime}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\{l_{j}:{\sf G}_{ij}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
imply

\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\&{l_{j}}}\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\{{\sf G}_{ij}\}_{j\in J_{i},i\in I})
for all
j\in\bigcup_{i\in I}J_{i}
.

The projection graph
\mathbb{G}^{\mathsf{proj}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}({\sf G})
is the graph reachable from the initial node. If it is not a valid local type graph,
then the projection is undefined.

Note that all nodes are of the form

\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{(\mathfrak{g})}
for some set of global types
\mathfrak{g}
.
Intuitively, a node
\mathfrak{g}
in the subset construction
\mathbb{G}^{\mathsf{proj}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}({\sf G})
represents all possible states in which
the global protocol could be in, from the perspective of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
.
Rule
[
G-End
]
allows
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
to terminate given that it is not possible for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
to be involved in any further communications.
Rules
[
G-Out
]
,
[
G-In
]
and
[
G-Sel
]
allow
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
to communicate if all possible communications are identical.
Rule
[
G-Bra
]
additionally accommodates for the full merge by
becoming looser with labels.
Example
C.16
shows how the subset construction
is built.

Theorem 4.28 (Soundness of the subset construction).

(
Proof in
Appendix
C.4
)
Let
{\sf G}
be a balanced global type.
If the projection graph
\mathbb{G}^{\mathsf{proj}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}({\sf G})
is defined,
then
{\sf G}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\mathbb{G}^{\mathsf{proj}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}({\sf G})
.

Proof.

By proving
\mathcal{R}=\{({\sf G}^{\prime},\mathbb{G}(\mathscr{C}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}(\{{\sf G}^{\prime}\})))\ |\ {\sf G}^{\prime}\in{\rm{Sub}}({\sf G})\}
is a subset of relation
\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
.
∎

Completeness is proved by contradiction,
assuming that
{\sf G}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{\sf T}
but some local type
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{G}^{\mathsf{proj}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}({\sf G})
is invalid (i.e. its outgoing edges do not match any of the rules in the subset construction).

Theorem 4.29 (Completeness of the subset
construction).

(
Proof in
Appendix
C.4
)
Let
{\sf G}
be a balanced global type such that
{\sf G}\upharpoonright_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{\sf T}
. Then the subset construction
\mathbb{G}^{\mathsf{proj}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}({\sf G})
is defined.

Theorem 4.30.

The coinductive full projectability problem for global types is in
PSPACE
.

Proof.

In polynomial space, we can check all reachable subsets from the initial node of the subset construction, and check that the resulting transitions form a correct type graph.
Then the result follows from the soundness and completeness of the subset construction.
∎

Theorem 4.31.

Finding a coinductive projection of a balanced global type
|{\sf G}|
onto a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
with full merging takes time
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left({|{\sf G}|}\cdot 2^{{|{\sf G}|}}\right)
.

Proof.

We can use the subset construction: there are at most
2^{|{\sf G}|}
nodes in the projection graph, and each node can be generated in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|{\sf G}|\right)
time. ∎

5.
Minimum Type Inference and its Complexity Analysis

This section first gives a quick summary of
the top-down typing system from
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)

and develops a
minimum type inference system
,
and gives its complexity analysis.
The term “minimum” means the smallest type
P
can have with respect
to the subtyping relation (following the same term in
(
Abadi and Cardelli, 1996
)
).

5.1.
Top Down Type Checking

For the top-down procedure (Figure
1(a)
),
a typing system uses a projection of global types to type a multiparty session
M
. Let us define a
typing environment

(
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\dots
) as
\Gamma::=\emptyset~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\Gamma,x{:}S~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\Gamma,X{:}{\sf T}
, and a
(session) typing context

\Delta::={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{:}{\sf T}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
(notice that there should be at least one participant in
M
). We write
\Delta({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}})

for
{\sf T}
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{:}{\sf T}\in\Delta
and denote
\operatorname{dom}({\Delta})
.
The typing judgments of expressions and processes are defined as

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:S
and
\Gamma\vdash P:{\sf T}
, respectively,
as defined in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
.

Following
(
Barwell et al., 2023
;
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
)
,
to type a multiparty session
M
, we associate a typing context
\Delta

to a global type
{\sf G}
via projection and subtyping.

Definition 5.1 (Association, Definition 10 in
(
Barwell et al., 2023
)
and
Definition 9 in
(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
)
).

A
typing context
\Delta
is associated with a global type
{\sf G}
,
written
\Delta\sqsubseteq{\sf G}
if
\operatorname{dom}({\Delta})={\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}
and
(1)
\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}
,
\Delta({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}})\leqslant{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
where

{{\sf G}}\!\!\upharpoonright\!{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
is defined by the inductive projection
(Def.
4.5
) with
the plain merge (Def.
4.6
) or
the full merge (Def.
4.7
); or (2)

\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}
,
\Delta({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}})\leqslant{\sf T}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}

such that
{{\sf G}}\!\!\downharpoonleft_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{{\sf T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
where

{{\sf G}}\!\!\downharpoonleft_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{{\sf T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}
is defined by the coinductive projection
(Def.
4.13
) with
the plain merge (Def.
4.14
) or
the coinductive projection with the full merge
(Def.
4.23
) with
{\sf G}
balanced.

Notice that the association enlarges a typing context beyond
directly projected local types.
Recall
\Delta_{5}
in the table in
Figure
4
and

{\sf G}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\},l_{4}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}\}
. Then

\Delta_{5}\sqsubseteq{\sf G}
.
Figure
5
lists the rules for processes.
The rules for expressions are standard and found in
Appendix
D.1
.
The top-down typing of
M
is defined as

\vdash^{\mathtt{T}}M::\Delta
by
[
T-Sess
]
where
a set of participants are typed by a typing context
\Delta

associated by a global type
(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
)
.

Processes
\Gamma\vdash P:{\sf T}

[
T-Sub
]

\Gamma\vdash P:{\sf T}
{\sf T}\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
\Gamma\vdash P:{\sf T}^{\prime}

[
T-Inact
]

\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{0}:\mathtt{end}

[
T-Rec
]

\Gamma,X:{\sf T}\vdash P:{\sf T}
\Gamma\vdash\mu X.P:{\sf T}

[
T-Var
]

X:{\sf T}\in\Gamma
\Gamma\vdash X:{\sf T}

[
T-In
]

\Gamma,x:S\vdash P:{\sf T}
\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).P:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}

[
T-Out
]

\Gamma\vdash P:{\sf T}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:S
\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}

[
T-Bra
]

\forall i\in I.\>\Gamma\vdash P_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}

[
T-Sel
]

\Gamma\vdash P:{\sf T}
\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleleft l.P:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l:{\sf T}\}

[
T-Cond
]

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{bool}
\Gamma\vdash P:{\sf T}
\Gamma\vdash P^{\prime}:{\sf T}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P~\mathsf{else}~P^{\prime}:{\sf T}

Session
\vdash M:\Delta

[
T-Sess
]

\forall i\in I.\ \vdash P_{i}:\Delta({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i})
{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}\subseteq\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\sqsubseteq{\sf G}
\vdash^{\mathtt{T}}\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}::P_{i}\triangleright\Delta

[
B-Sess
]

\forall i\in I.\ \vdash P_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
\mathsf{safe}{(\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}{:}{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I})}
\vdash^{\mathtt{B}}\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}::P_{i}\triangleright\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}{:}{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}

Figure 5
.
Typing system for processes and a session (
[
T-Sess
]
top-down
and
[
B-Sess
]
bottom-up)

The system satisfies the subject reduction theorem (
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
, Theorem 3.21)
), and
by construction
,
a typed multiparty session
M
automatically
satisfies desired properties defined in
Definition
2.1

(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
, Theorem 7)
using any of
the four projections.
The following theorems are proved in
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
;
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
;
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
.

Theorem 5.2 (Top-down soundness).

Suppose
\vdash^{\mathtt{T}}M\triangleright\Delta
by
[
T-Sess
]
.
Then
M
is communication safe, deadlock-free and live.

Complexity of the top-down system

Since checking
{\mathtt{pt}}\{{\sf G}\}\subseteq\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}\mid i\in I\}

is linear, the complexity of the top-down approach is
a sum of the complexity of (i) projection and
(iii-a) type checking of
P
against (ii) a subtype of a projection
of
{\sf G}
(Figure
1(a)
).
In the case of coinductive projection,
we additionally require checking
{\sf G}
is balanced.
As in Figure
1(b)
,
(iii-a) and (ii) are reduced to (ii-b) the minimum type inference
and (iii) the subtype checking.
The complexity of (2-2) is given in
Theorems
3.11

and
3.13
.
The rest of this selection develops
the minimum type inference and analyses its complexity.

5.2.
Minimum Type Inference System

Overview.

The minimum type inference system of process
P
is constructed in
two steps:
first, we build the
constraint inference system

for generating a constraint set of subtyping relations
of
P
;
then we try to solve the constraint set to obtain the minimum type
of
P
. More precisely:

(1)

We construct
the constraint inference system
(Definition
5.4
) for deriving a constraint set
\mathcal{C}
for process
P
.

(2)

We prove that the constraint inference system satisfies:

(a)

soundness
(Theorem
5.10
):
if a constraint set

\mathcal{C}
is derived from
P
, then
P
is typable
by a solution of
\mathcal{C}
; and

(b)

completeness
:
(Theorem
5.11
):
if
P
is typable by type
{\sf T}
and a constraint set

\mathcal{C}
is derived from
P
, then there exists a solution of
\mathcal{C}

which corresponds to
{\sf T}
.

(3)

We define the algorithm to find the
minimum type graph

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
a set of sort constraints
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
given
P
and
\mathcal{C}
(Definition
5.15
);

(4)

We prove that the algorithm satisfies:

(a)

soundness
(Theorem
5.21
): the most general
solution
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
generated
with
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
by the algorithm corresponds to the solution of
\mathcal{C}
; and

(b)

completeness

(Theorem
5.22
):
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}

substituted by the most general solution of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
is a type graph of
the minimum type of
P
.

In this subsection, we define (
1
) and prove
(
2
).
The second part
(
3
)–(
4
) will be
presented in
\mathsection
5.3
.

We start by introducing the definition of
minimum types
.

Definition 5.3 (Minimum types).

A
minimum type
of a process
P
is a type
{\sf T}
such that:
(1)
\emptyset\vdash P:{\sf T}
; and
(2) for all
{\sf T}^{\prime}
such that
\emptyset\vdash P:{\sf T}^{\prime}
, there exists a sort substitution
\pi:\mathcal{S}_{\text{v}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
such that
{\sf T}\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}\pi
.

We now introduce
the
constraints
for
unification

(
Pierce, 2002
, Chapter 22.3)
. First, we extend the syntax of types

{\sf T}
to include a countable set of
type variables

\mathcal{T}_{\text{v}}
, denoted by
\xi,\psi,\dots
;
we also extend sorts
S
with a countable set of
sort variables

\mathcal{S}_{\text{v}}
,
ranged over
\alpha,\beta,\dots
.
A
constraint set

\mathcal{C}
includes elements of the form
{\sf T}\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
and

S=S^{\prime}
.

Given a constraint set
\mathcal{C}
on a set of free variables
\mathcal{X}
,
a
type substitution

\sigma:\mathcal{T}_{\text{v}}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{T}
is a partial function from type variables to types.
Similarly, we define sort substitutions
\pi:\mathcal{S}_{\text{v}}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{S}
.
We extend these substitutions to act on types and sorts respectively:
\sigma:\mathcal{T}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{T}
and
\pi:\mathcal{S}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{S}
.

Definition 5.4 (Constraint inference).

Define
contexts

\Gamma::=\emptyset\ |\ \Gamma,x:\alpha\ |\ \Gamma,X:\xi
. Let us write
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}
for disjoint union and alpha-convert all
\mu
-bound variables to be unique.
Figure
6
defines the judgements for rules
for process
P
and selected rules for expression
e
:

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}

and
\Gamma\vdash P:\psi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}

which state that
(1) any solution for
\mathcal{C}
substituted into
\alpha

gives a type for
\mathsf{e}
; and
(2) any solution for
\mathcal{C}
substituted into
\psi

gives a type for
P
.

Note that each judgement in the proof tree must type a process
or an expression as a single type variable.
In each rule we combine all the constraints
\mathcal{C}
from the premises,
and add all fresh variables to
\mathcal{X}
.
Explanations of the rules for expressions are found in
Appendix
D.2
.
Rule
[
C-End
]
states that a nil must be typed as a supertype of
\mathtt{end}
.
Rules
[
C-Var
]
and
[
C-Rec
]
are used for recursions: the type of a recursive process must be a subtype of the type of its corresponding
recursion variable.
In rule
[
C-In
]
, we assume that
x
has sort
\alpha
and so the input must have a payload of
\alpha
.
In rule
[
C-Out
]
,
the expression must have the same sort as the payload of the type.
Rules
[
C-Bra
]
and
[
C-Sel
]
ensure that the type variable has valid types with respect to each branch.
Rule
[
C-Cond
]
verifies that the expression is of sort
\mathtt{bool}
and the two sides of the conditional statement have the same type.

Expressions
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}

[
C-SortVar
]

x:\alpha\in\Gamma
\Gamma\vdash x:\alpha\mid_{\emptyset}\emptyset

[
C-True
]

\alpha\text{ fresh}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{true}:\alpha\mid_{\{\alpha\}}\{\alpha=\mathtt{bool}\}

[
C-False
]

\alpha\text{ fresh}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{false}:\alpha\mid_{\{\alpha\}}\{\alpha=\mathtt{bool}\}

[
C-Nat
]

\alpha\text{ fresh}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{n}:\alpha\mid_{\{\alpha\}}\{\alpha=\mathtt{nat}\}

[
C-Int
]

\alpha\text{ fresh}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{i}:\alpha\mid_{\{\alpha\}}\{\alpha=\mathtt{int}\}

[
C-Neg
]

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}\cup\{\alpha=\mathtt{int}\}
\Gamma\vdash{\tt neg}(\mathsf{e}):\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

[
C-Not
]

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}\cup\{\alpha=\mathtt{bool}\}
\Gamma\vdash\neg\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

[
C-Or
]

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}:\alpha_{1}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{1}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{2}:\alpha_{2}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}\mathcal{C}_{2}
\beta\text{ fresh}
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\{\alpha_{1}=\mathtt{bool},\alpha_{2}=\mathtt{bool},\beta=\mathtt{bool}\}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}\vee\mathsf{e}_{2}:\beta\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathcal{X}_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\beta\}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

[
C-Nondet
]

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}:\alpha_{1}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{1}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{2}:\alpha_{2}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}\mathcal{C}_{2}
\beta\text{ fresh}
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\{\alpha_{1}=\beta,\alpha_{2}=\beta\}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{e}_{2}:\beta\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathcal{X}_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\beta\}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

[
C-Add
]

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}:\alpha_{1}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{1}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{2}:\alpha_{2}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}\mathcal{C}_{2}
\beta\text{ fresh}
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\{\alpha_{1}=\beta,\alpha_{2}=\beta,\beta=\mathtt{int}\}
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}+\mathsf{e}_{2}:\beta\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathcal{X}_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\beta\}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

C-Bra

\forall i\in I.\>\Gamma\vdash P_{i}:\psi_{i}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}\mathcal{C}_{i}

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}_{i}\cup\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\psi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\leqslant\xi\}
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits_{i\in I}\mathcal{X}_{i}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\xi\}
\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

[
C-Sel
]

\Gamma\vdash P:\psi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\xi\}

Example 5.5 (Constraint inference of a conditional).

Let
P=\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{true}~\mathsf{then}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l_{2}.{\mathbf{0}}),\ l_{3}:\mathbf{0}\}~\mathsf{else}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l_{4}.{\mathbf{0}}),\ l_{5}:\mathbf{0}\}
.
This process should have a type

{\sf T}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end},l_{4}:\mathtt{end}\}\}
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}=\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{2}:\xi_{1}\}\leqslant\xi_{2}\}
,

\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{3},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\xi_{2},l_{3}:\xi_{3}\}\leqslant\xi_{4}\}
,

\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}=\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{5},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{4}:\xi_{5}\}\leqslant\xi_{6}\}
,

\mathcal{C}_{2}=\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}\cup\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{7},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\xi_{6},l_{5}:\xi_{7}\}\leqslant\xi_{8}\}
.

(a)=

[
C-Bra
]

[
C-End
]

[
C-Sel
]

[
C-End
]

\vdash\mathbf{0}:\xi_{1}\mid_{\{\xi_{1}\}}\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{1}\}

\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l_{2}:\mathbf{0}.:\xi_{2}\mid_{\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}

\vdash\mathbf{0}:\xi_{3}\mid_{\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\}}\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{3}\}

\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l_{2}:\mathbf{0}.,l_{3}:\mathbf{0}\}:\xi_{4}\mid_{\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}\}}\mathcal{C}_{1}

(b)=

C-Bra

[
C-End

[

[
C-End

\vdash\mathbf{0}:\xi_{5}\mid_{\{\xi_{5}\}}\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{1}\}

\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l_{4}:\mathbf{0}.:\xi_{6}\mid_{\{\xi_{5},\xi_{6}\}}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}

\vdash\mathbf{0}:\xi_{7}\mid_{\{\xi_{5},\xi_{6},\xi_{7}\}}\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{3}\}

\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\triangleleft l_{4}:\mathbf{0}.,l_{5}:\mathbf{0}\}:\xi_{8}\mid_{\{\xi_{5},\xi_{6},\xi_{7},\xi_{8}\}}\mathcal{C}_{2}
