C-Cond
]

[
C-True
]

(\ref{ex:constraint_derivation_of_process_1:num1})
(\ref{ex:constraint_derivation_of_process_1:num2})
\vdash\mathsf{true}:\alpha\mid_{\{\alpha\}}\{\mathtt{bool}=\alpha\}

\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4},\xi_{5},\xi_{6},\xi_{7},\xi_{8},\alpha,\xi\}}\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\{\mathtt{bool}=\alpha,\xi_{4}\leqslant\xi,\xi_{8}\leqslant\xi\}

The last derived constraint set is
\mathcal{C}=\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{2}:\xi_{1}\}\leqslant\xi_{2},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{3},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\xi_{2},l_{3}:\xi_{3}\}\leqslant\xi_{4},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{5},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{4}:\xi_{5}\}\leqslant\xi_{6},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{7},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\xi_{6},l_{5}:\xi_{7}\}\leqslant\xi_{8},\mathtt{bool}=\alpha,\xi_{4}\leqslant\xi,\xi_{8}\leqslant\xi\}
.
Then
(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}
with

\sigma=\{\xi\mapsto{\sf T},\xi_{1}\mapsto\mathtt{end},\xi_{2}\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\},\xi_{3}\mapsto\mathtt{end},\xi_{4}\mapsto{\sf T},\xi_{5}\mapsto\mathtt{end},\xi_{6}\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{4}:\mathtt{end}\},\xi_{7}\mapsto\mathtt{end},\xi_{8}\mapsto{\sf T}\}

and
\pi=\{\alpha\mapsto\mathtt{bool}\}
.
Hence
\xi\pi\sigma={\sf T}
, so by soundness (will be proven in
Theorem
5.10
), we derive
\vdash P:{\sf T}
.

Example 5.6 (Constraint derivation of a process (2)).

Let
P=\mu X.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle x\rangle.X
. Then the constraint derivation of
P
is the following:

[
C-Rec
]

[
C-In
]

[
C-Out
]

[
C-SortVar
]

[
C-Var
]

X:\xi,x:\alpha\vdash X:\xi_{1}\mid_{\{\xi_{1}\}}\{\xi\leqslant\xi_{1}\}

X:\xi,x:\alpha\vdash x:\alpha\mid_{\emptyset}\emptyset

X:\xi,x:\alpha\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle x\rangle.X:\xi_{2}\mid_{\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}}\{\xi\leqslant\xi_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi_{1}\leqslant\xi_{2}\}

X:\xi\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle x\rangle.X:\xi\mid_{\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\alpha\}}\mathcal{C}

\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\{\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\alpha\}}\mathcal{C}

where the generated set of constraints
\mathcal{C}=\{\xi\leqslant\xi_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi_{1}\leqslant\xi_{2},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi_{2}\leqslant\xi\}
.

(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}
where
\sigma=\{\xi\mapsto\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\mathbf{t},\xi_{1}\mapsto\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\mathbf{t},,\xi_{2}\mapsto\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\mathbf{t}\}
and
\pi=\{\alpha\mapsto\mathtt{int},\beta\mapsto\mathtt{int}\}
.
Soundness implies that
\vdash P:{\sf T}
where
{\sf T}=\xi\pi\sigma=\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle;\mathbf{t}
; however, note that
{\sf T}
is not the only type of
P
(e.g.
\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\mathtt{bool}}\rangle;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\mathtt{bool}}\rangle;\mathbf{t}
is another type of
P
).

Definition 5.7 (Solutions).

Given that
\mathcal{C}
is a constraint set, we say that (
\sigma,\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}
if for all
\xi\leqslant\psi\in\mathcal{C}
,
\xi\sigma\pi\leqslant\psi\sigma\pi
, and for all
\alpha=\beta\in\mathcal{C}
,
\alpha\pi=\beta\pi
.

Lemma 5.8 (Uniqueness of constraints).

(
Proof in
Appendix
D.3
)
Given a process
P
, there is a unique constraint set
\mathcal{C}
(up to renaming) such that
\Gamma\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
for some
\Gamma,\xi,\mathcal{X}
. That is, if
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
are two such sets, then there exists one-to-one
\sigma:\mathcal{S}_{\text{v}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\text{v}}
,
\pi:\mathcal{T}_{\text{v}}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{\text{v}}
such that
\mathcal{C}\sigma\pi=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By inversion, there is only one rule that can be used for any
P
, thus the trees have the same shape.
We map the fresh variables from one tree to the corresponding
fresh variables in the other.
We prove this by induction on the proof trees of

\Gamma\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}

and
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash P:\xi^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 5.9.

A constraint generated from the rules in
Definition
5.4
is of the form of
either
\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi
,
\xi\leqslant\psi
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\beta}\rangle;\xi\leqslant\psi
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\beta}\rangle;\xi\leqslant\psi
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\leqslant\psi
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\leqslant\psi
,
or
S=S^{\prime}
for
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
;
and the right-hand side type variables (e.g.
\psi
in

{\sf T}\leqslant\psi
) in
\mathcal{C}
are pairwise distinct.

Theorem 5.10 (Soundness of constraints).

(
Proof in
Appendix
D.3
)
If
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
is derivable and
(\sigma,\pi)

is a solution to
\mathcal{C}
, then
P
has type
\xi\sigma\pi
.

Proof.

We show that if
\Gamma\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
is derivable and
(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}
,
then there is a typing derivation for
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:\xi\sigma\pi

(where we define
\Gamma\sigma\pi
to apply
\sigma\pi
to each type in
\Gamma
).
For sorts, we prove: if
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}

is derivable,
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\sigma\pi
.
We prove these by induction on the proof tree of the constraint derivations.
∎

Theorem 5.11 (Completeness of constraints).

(
Proof in
Appendix
D.3
)
Let
\vdash P:{\sf T}_{0}
and
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
. Then there exists a solution
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\xi\sigma^{\prime}\pi^{\prime}={\sf T}_{0}
.

Proof.

We show that if
\Gamma\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
, and
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:{\sf T}_{0}
, such that
\operatorname{dom}({\sigma})\cap\mathcal{X}=\emptyset
and
\operatorname{dom}({\pi})\cap\mathcal{X}=\emptyset
,
then there exists a solution
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\xi\sigma^{\prime}\pi^{\prime}={\sf T}_{0}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\sigma
, and
\pi^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\pi
.
We also show the analogous statement for sorts. The proof is done by
postponing the application of
[
T-Sub
]
in the derivations of

P
.
∎

5.3.
Solving the Minimum Types

This section proves that there exists an algorithm to find the
minimum type for typable
P
((
3
) in

\mathsection

5
). The algorithm constructs
a
minimum type graph
(denoted by
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
)
and
sort constraints

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}

with respect to
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
.
We then prove
soundness
(
4a
) and

completeness
(
4b
) of the algorithm, deriving the minimum type of
P
as a type graph instantiated by the most general
solution of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
.

Definition 5.12 (Most general solutions for sorts).

Let
\pi
solve the sort constraints
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
if
S_{1}\pi=S_{2}\pi
for all
S_{1}=S_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
.
Then
\pi
is a most general solution of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
if, for all
\pi^{\prime}
solving
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
,
there exists
\pi^{\prime\prime}
such that
\alpha\pi^{\prime}=\alpha\pi\pi^{\prime\prime}
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{S}_{\text{v}}
.

Note that, given a set of sort constraints, we may use a union-find to identify the equivalence classes of equality constraints. Then we can find the most general solution of the constraints using these equivalence classes.

Definition 5.13.

Define
\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})
to be a
minimum constraint set

created by the following procedure: while
\xi\leqslant\psi
occurs in
\mathcal{C}
,
perform the substitution
[\xi/\psi]
on
\mathcal{C}
.

Below Lemma
5.14
tells us that it is sufficient to find a solution to
\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})
, which can be used to reconstruct a solution to
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 5.14.

For constraint set
\mathcal{C}
created from Definition
5.4
containing
\xi\leqslant\psi
, if
(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}[\xi/\psi]
then
(\sigma_{[\psi\mapsto\sigma(\xi)]},\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

By considering each rule in
\mathcal{C}
, and using transitivity of subtyping.
∎

We now define the algorithm to generate a pair of
the minimum type graph
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}

and sort constraint
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
from
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
.
Intuitively, each node in the minimum type graph corresponds to the set of

expected behaviours
of the process.

Definition 5.15 (Minimum type graph).

Let
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in\wp(\mathcal{T}_{\text{v}})
denote
a subset of type variables.
The minimum type graph
(denoted by
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
)
with
the constraint

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
of

\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{C}=\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})

is a directed connected graph where
(1) a node
is a subset of type variables in
\mathcal{C}
or
\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}
; and
(2) the edges are given
by the transition
\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}
defined below.

A sort constraint is generated from
\{\xi\}

starting from the initial constraint

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}=\{S_{1}=S_{2}\ ~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\ S_{1}=S_{2}\in\mathcal{C}\}
.
New constratins will be added to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
when rule

[
M-IO
]
is applied.

The transition relation

\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}{\ell}}}
which generates
a type graph and the sort constraint
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
is defined below
(Lemma
5.9
constrains the possible
elements of
\mathcal{C}
).

•

[
M-End
]

If
\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{i}\in\mathcal{C}

for all
\xi_{i}\in\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
then
{\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}
.

•

[
M-IO
]

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\dagger\langle{\beta_{i}}\rangle;{\xi}_{i}\leqslant\psi_{k}\in\mathcal{C}
such that

\dagger\in\{!,?\}
for all
\psi_{k}\in\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\in K}
with
i\in I
,
then
{\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\in K}}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\dagger\langle{\alpha}\rangle}{\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
;
and update
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}\cup\{\alpha=\beta_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.

•

[
M-Sel
]

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{j}:{\xi_{ij}}\}_{j\in J_{i},i\in I}\leqslant\psi_{k}\in\mathcal{C}

for all
\psi_{k}\in\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\in K}
,
then
\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\in K}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{j}}}\{\xi_{ij}\}_{j\in J_{i},i\in I}
for each

j\in\bigcup_{i\in I}J_{i}
.

•

[
M-Bra
]

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{j}:{\xi_{ij}}\}_{j\in J_{i},i\in I}\leqslant\psi_{k}\in\mathcal{C}

for all
\psi_{k}\in\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\in K}
,
then
\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\in K}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{j}}}\{\xi_{ij}\}_{j\in J_{i},i\in I}
for each

j\in\bigcap_{i\in I}J_{i}
.

\{\xi\}
\{\xi_{2},\xi_{6}\}
\{\xi_{1}\}
\{\xi_{5}\}
\mathsf{Skip}
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{1}}
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{2}}
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{4}}
\mathsf{end}
\mathsf{end}

(a)

\{\xi\}
\{\xi_{2}\}
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\alpha_{1}}\rangle
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}!\langle{\alpha_{2}}\rangle

(b)

Figure 7
.
Minimum type graphs
of Example
5.16
(a)
and Example
5.17
(b)

The type graph for
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, denoted by

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})
is the graph reachable by the transitions from

\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.
If the generated type graph is invalid (i.e. not a type graph
of any local type),
it is
undefined
.

We explain the transition rules in Definition
5.15
.
Intuitively,
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a node that implements the behaviours of
\xi_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
Rule
[
M-End
]
means a node whose type variables are all supertypes of
\mathtt{end}
can be typed by
\mathtt{end}
;
rule
[
M-IO
]
defines
a transit with an input or output action with a fresh sort variable
\alpha
.
Sort constraints
\alpha=\beta_{i}
are added to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
to enforce that the payloads have the same types;
rule
[
M-Sel
]
adds the edge
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{j}}

for each
j
in the
union
of
J_{i}
. This is because
a supertype of the selection type has
more
choices so that all possible
edges should be generated; and rule
[
M-Bra
]
states its
dual–edge
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{j}}
for each
j

in the
intersection
of
J_{i}
should be generated.

Example 5.16 (Minimum type graph (1)).

We continue Example
5.5
. We have (up to renaming of type variables)

\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})=\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{2}:\xi_{1}\}\leqslant\xi_{2},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{3},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\xi_{2},l_{3}:\xi_{3}\}\leqslant\xi,\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{5},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{4}:\xi_{5}\}\leqslant\xi_{6},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{7},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\xi_{6},l_{5}:\xi_{7}\}\leqslant\xi,\mathtt{bool}=\alpha\}
.
Then the minimum type graph
\mathbb{G}_{\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})}
is given in Figure
7
.
This graph represents that after branching from the label
l_{1}
,
the process can be in either side of the conditional statement, thus it must respect the behaviours of both.
This is represented by the node being a two element-set, with one element corresponding to each selection.
After selecting the labels
l_{2}
or
l_{4}
, this nondeterminism is resolved, as each side of the conditional statement has a disjoint set of branches. Thus we have two separate nodes with one single element, which can now have different behaviours.

Formally, we have that the sort constraints are
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}=\{\alpha=\mathtt{bool}\}
.
A most general solution is
\pi=\{\alpha\mapsto\mathtt{bool}\}
, so the minimum type is the type corresponding to
\mathbb{G}_{\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})}
, with
\pi
applied to each transition:

{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end},l_{4}:\mathtt{end}\}\}
.

Example 5.17 (Minimum type graph (2)).

We continue Example
5.6
. We have (up to renaming of type variables)
\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi\leqslant\xi_{2},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi_{2}\leqslant\xi\}
.
Its minimum type graph is given in Figure
7
(b).
The sort constraints are
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}=\{\alpha=\alpha,\alpha=\alpha_{1},\alpha=\alpha_{2}\}
.
The most general solution is
\pi=\{\alpha\mapsto\alpha,\alpha_{1}\mapsto\alpha,\alpha_{2}\mapsto\alpha\}
.
Thus the minimum type is the type corresponding to the graph

\mathbb{G}_{\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})}
with
\pi
applied to the transitions:
{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}=\mu\mathbf{t}.{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\mathbf{t}
.

Example
5.18
shows how the system works
for the case of an untypable process.

Example 5.18 (Untypable process).

Let
P={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleleft l.\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{false}~\mathsf{then}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle 1\rangle.\mathbf{0}~\mathsf{else}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).\mathbf{0}
. This process is not typable because the the
branches of the conditional statement are not instances of a common type.
The constraints are
\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})=\{\alpha_{1}=\mathtt{bool},\alpha_{2}=\mathtt{int},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha_{2}}\rangle;\xi_{1}\leqslant\xi_{3},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha_{3}}\rangle;\xi_{2}\leqslant\xi_{3},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l:\xi_{3}\}\leqslant\xi\}
,
with
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
.
If we try to build a minimum type graph,
it fails at

\{\xi\}
\{\xi_{3}\}
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l}

since
\{\xi_{3}\}
does not meet the condition of
[
M-IO
]
in
Definition
5.15
, hence
the minimum type graph is undefined. Therefore no minimum type exists.

The following lemma states that set inclusion of type variables
in nodes of the minimum type graph gives rise to the subtyping relation
of types.

Lemma 5.19 (Subsets as subtyping).

Let
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
be a minimum type graph and
\pi
be a solution of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
generated with
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Suppose
\{\xi_{k}\}_{k\in K}
is a node in
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Then for all non-empty
I,J

such that
I\subseteq J\subseteq K
,

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})\pi\lesssim\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in J})\pi
. I.e.,

{\sf T}_{1}\pi\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}\pi
where
{\sf T}_{I}
and

{\sf T}_{2}
correspond to types of the type graphs

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})
and

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\in J})
, respectively.

Example 5.20 (Type graphs and subtyping).

We demonstrate that the subset relation
\subseteq
of variables at a node
corresponds to the subtyping relation
\lesssim
of types with two examples.

(1) Selection subtyping:
Consider the node
\{\xi_{2},\xi_{6}\}
in
Figure
7
(a) (Example
5.16
).
The type corresponding to

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{2},\xi_{6}\})
is

{\sf T}_{2,6}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end},l_{4}:\mathtt{end}\}
and
the type corresponding to

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{2}\})

is
{\sf T}_{2}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{2}:\mathtt{end}\}
. Hence

\{\xi_{2}\}\subseteq\{\xi_{2},\xi_{6}\}
implies

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{2}\})\lesssim\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{2},\xi_{6}\})

and
{\sf T}_{2}\leqslant{\sf T}_{2,6}
.

(2) Branching subtyping:
The following process with branchings demonstrates a subset of variables
produces
more
branchings (the opposite to the above selection example).
Consider

Q\ =\ \mathsf{if}~\mathsf{true}~\mathsf{then}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle 1\rangle.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:\mathbf{0},l_{3}:({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleleft l_{4}.\mathbf{0})\}~\mathsf{else}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle 1\rangle.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{2}:\mathbf{0},l_{3}:({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleleft l_{5}.\mathbf{0})\}

Then,
\vdash Q:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
with

\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C})=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha_{1}}\rangle;\xi_{1}\leqslant\xi,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\xi_{2},l_{3}:\xi_{3}\}\leqslant\xi_{1},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{2},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{4}:\xi_{4}\}\leqslant\xi_{3},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{4},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha_{2}}\rangle;\xi_{5}\leqslant\xi,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{2}:\xi_{6},l_{3}:\xi_{7}\}\leqslant\xi_{5},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{6},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{5}:\xi_{8}\}\leqslant\xi_{7},\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi_{8},\alpha_{1}=\mathtt{int},\alpha_{2}=\mathtt{int}\}
.
The minimum type graph of
Q
is given in
Figure
8
(a).

Now let

in the truth branch of
P
, which has type
{\sf T}_{1}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}{:}\mathtt{end},l_{3}{:}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{4}{:}\mathtt{end}\}\}
. Its type graph
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{1}\})
is given
in Figure

(b).
Consider
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{1},\xi_{5}\})
in

{\sf T}_{1,5}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{3}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{4}:\mathtt{end}\}\}
. Hence

\{\xi_{1}\}\subseteq\{\xi_{1},\xi_{5}\}
implies

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{1}\})\lesssim\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{1},\xi_{5}\})

and
{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{1,5}
.

Theorem 5.21 (Soundness of the minimum type inference).

(
Proof in
Appendix
D.4
)

Let
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
.
Let the minimum type graph be
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
with sort constraints
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
.
Let
\sigma=\{\psi\mapsto{\sf T}_{\psi}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~\psi\in\mathcal{C}\}
where
{\sf T}_{\psi}
is a type which corresponds to

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\psi\})
.
Assume
\pi
is a most general solution of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
.
Then
(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

All sort constraints are satisfied because
\pi
solves
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
which is a superset of the sort constraints of
\mathcal{C}
. We prove that all type constraints are satisfied by
\sigma\pi

using
\lesssim
. ∎

Below we write
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}\pi
to be the graph with
\pi
applied to all transitions.

Theorem 5.22 (Completeness of the minimum type inference).

(
Proof in
Appendix
D.4
)
Let
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{C}=\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
, such that
\mathcal{C}
is solvable.
Assume
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
are the minimum graph and sort constraints
of
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
.
If
\pi
is the most general solution to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
, then
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}\pi
is a type graph (Definition
3.6
) that corresponds to a minimum type (Definition
5.3
)
{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}
of
P
.

Proof.

Let
(\sigma,\pi)
be a solution to
\mathcal{C}
.
Let
{\sf T}=\xi\sigma\pi
.
By Theorems
5.10
and
5.11
, it suffices to show that there exists
\pi^{\prime\prime}
such that
{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}\pi^{\prime\prime}\leqslant{\sf T}
.
The proof involves defining a simulation
\prec
on

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})\pi^{\prime}
and
{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T})

inductively matching each subformula of
{\sf T}
with
corresponding type variables.
We show
\prec
is a type simulation proving two lemmas.
∎

Corollary 5.23.

If
\vdash P:{\sf T}
is derivable, then there exists a minimum type graph for
{\sf T}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
D.2
, there exists a derivation for
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
.
Thus, by Theorem
5.11
, there exists a solution
(\sigma,\pi)
of
\mathcal{C}
.
Therefore, a solution also exists for
\mathtt{tr}(C)
by Lemma
5.14
.
The result follows by Theorem
5.22
.
∎

5.4.
Complexity of Minimum Type Inference

Definition 5.24 (Size of expressions, processes and sessions).

The size of an expression

\mathsf{e}
, denoted
{|\mathsf{e}|}
is
defined as:

|\mathsf{true}|=|\mathsf{false}|=|\mathsf{n}|=|\mathsf{i}|=|x|=1
,

|\neg\mathsf{e}|=|{\tt neg}(\mathsf{e})|=|\mathsf{e}|+1
, and

|\mathsf{e}\vee\mathsf{e}^{\prime}|=|\mathsf{e}+\mathsf{e}^{\prime}|=|\mathsf{e}\oplus\mathsf{e}^{\prime}|=|\mathsf{e}|+|\mathsf{e}^{\prime}|+1
.

The size of a process

P
, denoted
|P|
,
is defined as:

|\mathbf{0}|=|X|=1
,

|\mu X.P|=|P|+1
,

|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).P|=|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P|=|P|+|\mathsf{e}|+1
,

|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleleft l.{P}|=|P|+1
, and

|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}|=\sum_{i\in I}|P_{i}|+1
, and

|\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P_{1}~\mathsf{else}~P_{2}|=|P_{1}|+|P_{2}|+|\mathsf{e}|+1
.

The size of a session

M
, denoted
{|M|}
,
is defined as:
{|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\,\mathbf{::}\,P|}={|P|}+1
,
and
{|M\ |\ M^{\prime}|}={|M|}+{|M^{\prime}|}+1
.

Lemma 5.25.

The number of judgements in a proof tree of
\Gamma\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
is
\leq|P|
and
|\mathcal{C}|
=
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|P|\right)
.

Proof.

By induction on the proof tree.
Note that we never unfold recursions in the judgement and the sum of the sizes of the processes in the premises is strictly less than the size of
P
.
Thus, the number of judgements in the proof tree is bounded by
|P|
.
We can also easily check
the number of constraints added per judgement in the proof tree
is bounded by a constant.
∎

Theorem 5.26.

The minimum type of a process
P
can be found
in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(n\cdot 2^{n}\right)
, where
n=|P|
.

Proof.

Finding the subset graph of Definition
5.15
requires
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(n\cdot 2^{n}\right)
time. First, building the set of constraints
\mathcal{C}
and finding
\mathtt{tr}(C)
take polynomial time in
n
, as there are only polynomially many type constraints.
The number of nodes in the proof tree for constraint derivation is at most
n
by Lemma
5.25
, and at most one new type variable is introduced per rule.
Therefore, there are
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(2^{n}\right)
nodes in the subset graph. The transitions outgoing from
\mathcal{S}
can be computed in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(n\right)
time, as the number of constraints is
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(n\right)
. Therefore, the total time complexity is
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(n\cdot 2^{n}\right)
.
∎

We can show an explicit example where the minimum type has an exponential size.

Theorem 5.27.

There exists a process
P
s. t. any minimum type of
P
has size exponential in
{|P|}
.

Proof.

Define
P_{n}=\mu X.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:\dots{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\triangleright\{l_{1}:X,l_{2}:X\}\dots\}\}\}
where
n
is the
number of
\&l_{1}
-nested branches (i.e.,
{|P_{n}|}=n+4
).
This has the minimum type
{\sf T}_{n}=\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{\dots{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{1}:\mathbf{t},l_{2}:\mathbf{t}\}\dots\}\}\}
where there are
n
number of
\&l_{1}
branch nested
constructs in
{\sf T}_{n}
(i.e.,
{|{\sf T}_{n}|}=n+4
).

Consider
P=\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P^{(1)}~\mathsf{else}~P^{(2)}
for some expression
\mathsf{e}
. If the minimum types of
P^{(1)}
and
P^{(2)}
are
{\sf T}_{n}
and
{\sf T}_{m}
respectively, then the minimum type of
P
is
{\sf T}_{\lcm(n,m)}
, as
l_{2}
is guaranteed to be safely received only when each cycle is synchronised.
Thus, for integers
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
, the process
P^{(k)}
, defined by

P^{(1)}=P_{n_{1}}
and

P^{(k)}=\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P_{n_{k}}~\mathsf{else}~P^{(k-1)}

has a minimum type of
{\sf T}_{\lcm(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})}
, which in general has size exponential in
\sum_{i\in\{1,\dots,k\}}n_{i}
.
∎

6.
Complexity Analysis of Bottom-Up MPST

6.1.
Bottom-Up Multiparty Session Type System

We now start the analysis of
bottom-up
MPST.
Recall a
typing context

\Delta
defined in
\mathsection
5.1
.
We often write
\Delta
as

\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}{:}{\sf T}_{i}
as shorthand for

\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{1}{:}{\sf T}_{1},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{n}{:}{\sf T}_{n}\}
where

\prod_{i\in\emptyset}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}{:}{\sf T}_{i}=\emptyset
.

Definition 6.1 (Size of a typing context).

Define
|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{:}{\sf T}|=|{\sf T}|
and
|\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}|=|\Delta|+|\Delta^{\prime}|
.

The typing system for process
P
does not change.
For typing a multiparty session, we use
[
B-Sess
]
instead of

[
T-Sess
]
where
\mathsf{safe}(\Delta)
means typing context
\Delta
satisfies
a
safety
property. To define this property, we first recall
the transition relation of typing contexts
from
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
, Definition 2.8)
.
The transition relation is similar to the LTS of the type graph
defined in Definition
3.6
.

Definition 6.2 (Labelled transition relation of typing contexts).

Define labels
(
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}^{\prime}\cdots
) by

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}::=\ {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\&{l}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\oplus{l}~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l~~\mathbf{|\!\!|}~~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}
.
Then the
labelled transition relation
(LTS) over typing contests,

\Delta\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime}
, is defined by:

[
E-IO
]

\dagger\in\{!,?\}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\dagger{S}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}

[
E-BS
]

\dagger\in\{\oplus,\&\}\hskip 9.24994ptk\in I
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\dagger{\{l_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\dagger{l_{k}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}_{k}

[
Comp
]

\Delta\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime}
\Delta,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}

[
Msg
]

\Delta_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle}\Delta^{\prime}_{1}
\Delta_{2}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle}\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\Delta^{\prime}_{2}

[
Bra
]

\Delta_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\oplus l}\Delta^{\prime}_{1}
\Delta_{2}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&l}\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l}\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\Delta^{\prime}_{2}

[
\mu
]

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}[\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}/\mathbf{t}]\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime}

Define
\Delta\rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
if
\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\Delta^{\prime}
or
\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l}\Delta^{\prime}

and
\Delta\rightarrow
(resp.
\Delta\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}
)
for

\exists\Delta^{\prime}.\Delta\rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
(resp.
\Delta\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime}
).

Definition 6.3 (Safety, Definition 4.1 in
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
).

•

\Delta
is a
safe state
if
(1)

\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle}
and
\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S^{\prime}}\rangle}
imply
\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}
; and
(2)

\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\oplus l}
and
\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&l^{\prime}}
imply
\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l}
.

•

\phi
is a
safety property
if, for all
\Delta
such that

\phi(\Delta)
, we have:
(1)

\Delta
is a safe state;
(2)

\Delta=\Delta^{\prime},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}
implies
\phi(\Delta^{\prime},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}[\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}/\mathbf{t}])
; and
(3)

\Delta\rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
implies
\phi(\Delta^{\prime})
.

We call

\Delta

safe
if
\phi(\Delta)
holds for some safety property
\phi
and write
\mathsf{safe}(\Delta)
if
\Delta
is safe.

Remark 6.1.

In Definition
6.3
,
the transition for selection and branching (2) is not symmetric;
in a safe state, all labels of
a selection
l
must be reducible, while
some

label of a branching needs to be reducible.
See
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
for more explanations.

Lemma 6.4.

(
Proof in
Appendix
E.2
)

\Delta
is safe if and only if
\Delta^{\prime}
is a safe state for all
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}
.

Theorem 6.5 (Communication safety, Corollary 4.7 in
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
).

Suppose
\vdash^{\mathtt{B}}M\triangleright\Delta
by
[
B-Sess
]
in
Figure
5
.
Then
M
is communication safe.

Unfortunately, under the bottom-up system,
typable
M
does not guarantee neither deadlock-freedom nor
liveness properties. To guarantee those properties for
M
,
one needs to
additionally
check
\Delta
satisfies
the corresponding property
\varphi(\Delta)
.
Below we recall the definitions from
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
;
Barwell et al., 2022
;
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
)
.

Definition 6.6 (Deadlock-freedom, Figure 5(2) in
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
).

\Delta
is deadlock-free if
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}\not\rightarrow
implies
{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}})=\mathtt{end}
for all
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in\Delta^{\prime}
.

Definition 6.7 (Fair and Live Paths, Definition 17 in
(
Barwell et al., 2022
)
).

A
path
is a sequence of contexts
\mathcal{P}::=(\Delta_{i})_{i\in N}
, where
N=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
is a (finite or
infinite) set of consecutive natural numbers, and

\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{i+1}
for all
i,i+1\in N
. Then a path
is
fair
if, for all
i\in N
,
\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}

implies
\exists j\geq i.\>\Delta_{j}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\Delta_{j+1}
, and

\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l}
implies
\exists l^{\prime},j
such that

j\geq i
and
\Delta_{j}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l^{\prime}}\Delta_{j+1}
.

A path is
live
if, for all
i\in N
:
(1)
\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle}
implies
\exists j\geq i.\>\Delta_{j}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\Delta_{j+1}
;
(2)
\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle}
implies
\exists j\geq i.\>\Delta_{j}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\Delta_{j+1}
;
(3)
\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\oplus l}
implies
\exists l^{\prime},j
such that
j\geq i
and
\Delta_{j}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l^{\prime}}\Delta_{j+1}
; and
(4)
\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\&l}
implies
\exists l^{\prime},j
such that
j\geq i
and
\Delta_{j}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:l^{\prime}}\Delta_{j+1}
.

Definition 6.8 (Liveness).

\Delta
is
live
, denoted
\mathsf{live}(\Delta)
,
if
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}
implies that all paths starting with
\Delta^{\prime}
that are fair are also live.

Remark 6.2 (Liveness).

We require fair paths to define liveness of typing contexts
(Definition
6.8
), while liveness of session
M

(Definition
2.1
)
does not require the fairness assumption.
This is because a typing context without the fair path requirement
can type
non-live sessions
. See
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
, Example 5.14)
and
(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
, Example 9)
.
More explanations with examples
can be found in
Appendix
E.1
.

Theorem 6.9 (Deadlock-freedom and liveness, Theorem 5.15 in
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
).

Suppose
\vdash^{\mathtt{B}}M\triangleright\Delta
with
\varphi\in\{\mathsf{df},\mathsf{live}\}
,
and
\varphi(\Delta)
. Then
M
satisfies
\varphi
.

Note that for all
\Delta
,
\mathsf{live}(\Delta)
implies

\mathsf{df}(\Delta)
but

\mathsf{live}(\Delta)
and
\mathsf{safe}(\Delta)
are incomparable, and

\mathsf{df}(\Delta)
and
\mathsf{safe}(\Delta)
are incomparable
(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
, Theorem 4)
.
See Figure
4
with appropriate examples.

Complexity of bottom-up system

The complexity of checking whether
M
satisfies

\varphi
(Figure
1(c)
)
is reduced to (i) the minimum type inference (given
in Theorem
5.26
)
and (ii) checking whether inferred
\Delta
satisfies
\varphi
.
For the rest of this section, we investigate the complexity of (ii).

6.2.
Checking Safety, Deadlock-Freedom and Liveness is in
PSPACE

Unfortunately, given typing context
\Delta
,
checking the three aforementioned properties is
PSPACE
-complete
.
We start proving that checking these properties
are in
PSPACE
.
First, we bound the number of reachable contexts
\Delta^{\prime}
such that
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}
by the product of the sizes of the types.

Lemma 6.10.

(
Proof in
Appendix
E.3
)
Let
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
. Then
|\{\Delta^{\prime}\mid\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}\}|\leq\prod_{i\in I}{|{\sf T}_{i}|}=2^{\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|\Delta|\right)}
where

\prod_{i\in I}n_{i}
denotes the product of
n_{i}
.

Thus the number of reachable contexts is at most exponential in the
size of the input (
\sum_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
). This gives rise to a
nondeterministic polynomial-space algorithm for checking the complement
of safety, where all states at a distance up to this exponential bound
are nondeterministically visited and checked for states that are not
safe.

Theorem 6.11.

(
Proof in
Appendix
E.3
)
Checking for safety and deadlock-freedom is in
PSPACE
.

Proof.

We construct a nondeterministic polynomial-space algorithm that accepts if and only if the input is not safe.
Given an initial context
\Delta
, the algorithm nondeterministically traverses a path of
\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
transitions or fewer,
finding a state
\Delta^{\prime}
such that
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}
. Then, it accepts iff
\Delta^{\prime}
is not a safe state.
Thus, there exists some accepting path iff there exists a reachable unsafe state by Theorem
6.10
.
Note that the path does not need to be explicitly stored; rather, only the current context needs to be stored (which can be done in polynomial space).

We use the fact that
\textsc{co-NPSPACE}=\textsc{PSPACE}
. Deadlock-freedom is similarly proved by checking a deadlocked state.
The algorithm (Algorithm
1
) is found in
Appendix
E.3
.
∎

The proof that checking for liveness is in
PSPACE
is more involved:
first, we define an equivalent characterisation of liveness. Live paths are characterised by
barbs
, which are the offered transitions in some context, and
observations
, which are the transitions that are matched in some reduction.

[
Barb-IO
]

\Delta\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\dagger{[S]}}\Delta^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\dagger\in\{!,?\}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\dagger\in\mathsf{barb}(\Delta)

[
Barb-BS
]

\Delta\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\dagger{l}}\Delta^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\dagger\in\{\oplus,\&\}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\&\in\mathsf{barb}(\Delta)

[
Obs-O
]

\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\Delta^{\prime}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}!\in\mathsf{obs}(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

[
Obs-I
]

\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}}\Delta^{\prime}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\in\mathsf{obs}(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

[
Obs-B
]

\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l}\Delta^{\prime}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\in\mathsf{obs}(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

[
Obs-S
]

\Delta\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l}\Delta^{\prime}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}\oplus\in\mathsf{obs}(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

Figure 9
.
Barbs
\mathsf{barb}(\Delta)
and observations
\mathsf{obs}(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})
.

Definition 6.12 (Barbs and observations).

Recall
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}
defined in Definition
6.2

and abbreviate

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}!
etc. if the payload is unimportant.
The sets of
barbs
of
\Delta
and
observations
of
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
are defined
in Figure
9
.
For paths
\mathcal{P}=(\Delta_{i})_{i\in N}
,
define
\mathsf{barb}(\mathcal{P})=\bigcup_{i\in N}\mathsf{barb}(\Delta_{i})

and
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{P})=\bigcup_{i,i+1\in N}\mathsf{obs}(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{i+1})
.

The set of barbs contains all actions offered by some type in
\Delta
, and the set of observations contains all barbs that become satisfied in the definition of liveness from some reduction (Definition
6.8
).
Note that
\mathsf{barb}
and
\mathsf{obs}
are checkable in linear time.
Then we define
counterwitness
, which is a path that is fair but not live.

Definition 6.13.

A path
(\Delta_{i})_{i\in N}
is a counterwitness of
\Delta
if
\Delta=\Delta_{0}
and
\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}}\Delta_{i+1}
and:

•

There exists
k\in N
and
a\in\mathsf{barb}(\Delta_{k})
such
that
a\not\in\mathsf{obs}((\Delta_{i})_{i\geq k})
; and

•

For all
k\in N
,
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid i\geq k\}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\mid\exists i\geq k.\>\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\in\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l\}\}
.

The proofs in the following lemmas are found in
Appendix
E.4
.

Lemma 6.14.

A counterwitness exists for
\Delta
if and only if
\Delta
is not live.

Counterwitnesses are paths which have a barb that is never observed.

Lemma 6.15.

A path
(\Delta_{i})_{i\in N}
is live iff
\mathsf{barb}(\Delta_{k})\subseteq\mathsf{obs}((\Delta_{i})_{i\geq k})
for all
k
.

Lemma 6.16.

Let
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
. Let
n=\sum_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
. Then there are at most
2n
possible reductions
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\in\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{q}}}}:l\}
such that
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}
.

Then, we give a sufficient bound on the length and periodicity of a counterwitness.

Lemma 6.17.

(
Proof in
Appendix
E.4
)
Let
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
. Let
m=(2n+2)\cdot\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
.
If
\Delta
is not live, then it has a finite counterwitness of size
k

such that

k\leq m
or an infinite counterwitness of the form
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\omega}
with
{|\mathcal{P}_{1}|}\leq m
and

{|\mathcal{P}_{2}|}\leq m
.

Theorem 6.18.

(
Proof in
Appendix
E.4
)
Checking for liveness is in
PSPACE
.

Proof.

Using the above bound on the length of a counterwitness, we can give a nondeterministic algorithm that checks for the complement of liveness by checking all paths in the form specified by Lemma
6.17
.
We construct a nondeterministic polynomial-space
algorithm that accepts if and only if the input is not live.
The algorithm nondeterministically chooses a candidate witness of the form
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\omega}
, where
{|\mathcal{P}_{1}|}\leq m

and
{|\mathcal{P}_{2}|}\leq m
such that
m=(2n+2)\cdot\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
.
Along the path, some
k\leq 2m
is nondeterministically chosen and the conditions in Definition
6.13
are checked.
A similar check is performed for finite witnesses of the form
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of length
j
such that
j\leq m
.
Along the path, the algorithm involves storing a finite number of sets of barbs, and there are only polynomially many barbs. Therefore, the algorithm is polynomial-space.
The algorithm (Algorithm
3
) is
found in Appendix
E.4
.
∎

Remark 6.3.

In practice, the number of reachable states is often much smaller than
the bound given in
Lemma
6.10
.
Hence even if the space complexity is not polynomial,
we can build a deterministic algorithm that might be more
practical with an explicit breadth-first search over all reachable
states.

Theorem 6.19.

Checking for safety (or deadlock-freedom) of a typing context
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
can be done in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|\Delta|\cdot U\right)
time
where
U=\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
.

We can also use a similar construction to the proof of Savitch’s theorem

(
Sipser, 2013
, Theorem 8.5)

to create a deterministic algorithm for checking liveness.

Theorem 6.20.

(
Proof in
Appendix
E.4.1
)

Let
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
and
U=\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
.
Then there exists a deterministic algorithm that checks if
\Delta
is live in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(U^{3}\cdot 2^{3|\Delta|}+U^{3}\log U\cdot 2^{2|\Delta|}\right)
time.

Remark 6.4.

If we restrict the number of participants to some constant
c
, then
Lemma
6.10
shows that the number of reachable configurations is
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(\prod_{i\in I}{|{\sf T}_{i}|}\right)=\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|\Delta|^{c}\right)
.
This implies that the algorithms described in Remark
6.3
are polynomial-time for a fixed number of participants,
and suggests that they are feasible
for larger typing contexts with a small number of participants.

6.3.
PSPACE-Hardness of Checking Safety, Deadlock-Freedom and Liveness

We turn our attention to
PSPACE
-hardness.
We reduce it from the
quantified Boolean formula (QBF)
problem
which is known to be
PSPACE
-complete
(
Sipser, 2013
, Theorem 8.9)
.
Given a formula in QBF
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{Q}_{1}x_{1}\dots\mathcal{Q}_{n}x_{n}\mathcal{F^{\prime}},\ \mathcal{Q}_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
, with
\mathcal{F^{\prime}}
in conjunctive normal form:
\mathcal{F^{\prime}}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}C_{i},\ C_{i}=(L_{i1}\vee L_{i2}\vee L_{i3})
where
L
is a literal which is either a variable
x_{i}
or its negation
\neg x_{i}
,
the QBF problem asks whether
\mathcal{F}
is true.
The full construction for the reduction from the QBF problem is very large and non-trivial:
we leave the detailed definitions and proofs in
Appendix
E.5
,
but give a summary.

Our construction is a typing context
\Delta_{\mathsf{init}}
that computes the truth value of
\mathcal{F}
. It contains one participant for each variable (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}
) and clause (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i}
), as well as the “controller” (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}
). Participant
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}
finds the truth value of the formula
\mathcal{Q}_{i}x_{i}\dots\mathcal{Q}_{n}x_{n}\mathcal{F^{\prime}}
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i}
finds the truth value of the clauses
C_{i}
onwards. The controller
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}
queries
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{1}
and enters an undesirable state
\mathsf{T}_{\rm{bad}}
if
\mathcal{F}
is false, otherwise the process repeats indefinitely.
We construct
\Delta_{\mathsf{init}}
such that its reduction paths are
deterministic
(i.e.,
\Delta\rightarrow\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta\rightarrow\Delta_{2}

imply
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}
) and
safe

(i.e.,
\Delta\rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
is safe if
\Delta
is a safe state).
This enables that all possible behaviours of the context are
analysed by the reductions from
\Delta_{\mathsf{init}}
, and those
reductions must not contain any violations of a property.

As safety and deadlock-freedom are reachability properties, we only need to exhibit a
\mathsf{T}_{\rm{bad}}
that violates the properties from
\Delta_{\mathsf{init}}
.
We prove that, if
\mathcal{F}
is true, then
\Delta_{\mathsf{init}}
reduces safely
and deterministically back to itself by the multistep reductions.
Otherwise
\Delta_{\mathsf{init}}
reduces to a state where
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}
has
the undesirable type
\mathsf{T}_{\rm{bad}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{1}\&\!\{\mathsf{unsafe}:\mathtt{end}\}
,
which represents an unsafe state.
For deadlock-freedom, we set
\mathsf{T}_{\rm{bad}}=\mathtt{end}
which creates a stuck state.

For the liveness, we first identify an equivalent formulation
of the live path (Definition
6.7
).
We then prove that if
\mathcal{F}
is satisfied, a deterministic infinite
live path reduces to the same state;
otherwise it leads to
\mathsf{T}_{\rm{bad}}=\mathtt{end}
showing the path is not live.
We can use the same bad type as deadlock-freedom since
if
\Delta
is live, it is deadlock-free.

Theorem 6.21.

(
Proof in
Appendices
E.6
,
E.7
,
E.8
.
)
Checking for safety, deadlock-freedom and liveness is
PSPACE
-hard.

Corollary 6.22.

Checking for safety, deadlock-freedom and liveness is
PSPACE
-complete.

7.

Summary.

We summarise the complexity results

\displaystyle highlighting
the algorithms and results we have constructed or proven
in this paper.
Table
1
lists the complexity of
the subtyping checking, including the complexity results from

(
Lange and Yoshida, 2016
;
Udomsrirungruang and Yoshida, 2024
)
(which will
be discussed in
Subtyping
in the related work).
Figure
4
lists
the relationship between the sets of local types
obtained by the projections and safety, deadlock-freedom and liveness
properties, and
Table
2
lists the complexity of the top-down approach for each projection.
In practice,
Figure
4
and Table
2
together give
us a stand-alone guidance of tractability in relation to
expressiveness
(i.e., a set of global types which the algorithm can project) since
local types are directly usable for implementing applications,
such as API generations and runtime-monitoring
(
Yoshida, 2024
;
Gay and Ravara, 2017
)
.
The inductive projections are lower complexity than the corresponding
coinductive projections.
The
tbc
algorithm is more efficient (quadratic) than
the coinductive projection with full merging (in
PSPACE
).
However,
tbc

completely
rejects
inductively projectable global types with full merging which are not inductively
projectable by plain merging (Proposition
4.21
).

The full complexity of type-checking with global type
{\sf G}
is given below.

Theorem 7.1 (Top-down full complexity).

Given
{\sf G}
and
M
, checking whether

\vdash^{\mathtt{T}}M:\Delta
such that
\Delta\sqsubseteq{\sf G}
is
exponential in
{|{\sf G}|}
and
{|M|}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.26
,
inferring the minimum type from
M
takes time
{|M|}\cdot 2^{|M|}

and generates a minimum type graph of size
2^{|M|}
. The complexities
of all projections in
\mathsection
4
are given in
Table
2
.
In the case of the coinductive projection
with full merging, we check
{\sf G}
is balanced, which costs
polynomial. Hence it does not
affect to the total complexity.
By summing up, the total complexity of type checking by all projection methods
are exponential in
{|{\sf G}|}+{|M|}
.
∎

Theorem 7.2 (Bottom-up full complexity).

Given
M
, checking whether there exits

\vdash^{\mathtt{B}}M:\Delta
where
\Delta
is safe,
deadlock or live has complexity exponential in
{|M|}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.26
, inferring the minimum type of
P

takes time
{|P|}\cdot 2^{|P|}

and generates a minimum type graph of size
2^{|P|}
.
Table
3
summarises the complexity of checking safety,
deadlock-freedom and liveness of the inferred typing context, respectively.
For a multiparty session
M=\prod_{i\in I}P_{i}
,
we have
U=\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}_{i}|\leq 2^{{|\sum_{i\in I}P_{i}|}}=\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(2^{|M|}\right)
.
Furthermore,
{|\Delta|}=\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(2^{{|M|}}\right)
.
Although the type inference and property-checking are individually exponential,
the total complexity in Table
3
is not doubly exponential, as the product of the minimum types
U
is exponential in
{|M|}
.
∎

While both approaches are exponential,
the bottom-up requires a more complex approach
during property-checking, and thus the algorithms
have a higher complexity. Among them, liveness is the highest.
In practice, it is unlikely that type inference yields types of
exponential size.
Ignoring the inference step,
but focusing on the
type-level
,
the top-down inductive plain and full merging and coinductive plain
merging approaches
are only
polynomial
in the size of the local types, while
the top-down coinductive full merging and
the bottom-up approaches
are
exponential
.
These suggest that the top-down approaches with polynomial complexity
are more efficient, albeit limited.

The top-down takes time linear in the size of the type graph of the projection. In practice,
the size of this type graph is much smaller than the theoretical bound of
2^{{|{\sf G}|}}
.
Even though examples exist where the projection has an exponential size (Theorem
4.24
),
these examples remain contrived and unnatural.
In the bottom-up approach, the complexity is
linear in the number of reachable typing contexts.
In practice,
this number can easily become exponential in the size of the session,
for example by composing many copies of independently interacting sessions.
This is the main source of the high complexity of the bottom-up approach.
For processes with a large number of participants and nondeterministic behaviour,
the bottom-up may be more expensive in practice.

Related and Future Work

Subtyping.

Gay and Hole
(
Gay and Hole, 1999
)
introduced
the binary synchronous session subtyping system and proposed
a sound and complete inductive subtyping algorithm in
(
Gay and Hole, 2005
)
.
Lange and Yoshida
(
Lange and Yoshida, 2016
)
reduce subtyping of synchronous binary session types to checking a modal
\mu
-calculus formula, and benchmark
their algorithm against
(
Gay and Hole, 2005
)
and a coinductive algorithm.
Ghilezan
et al.

(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
prove operational and
denotational
preciseness
of synchronous multiparty session
subtyping
for the synchronous multiparty session calculus.
An inductive subtyping algorithm is given in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
, but
they did not measure its complexity.
In this paper, we
have proven exponential in Theorem
3.11
.
We proposed a subtyping algorithm for the multiparty setting based on
a simulation extending the binary algorithm given in

(
Udomsrirungruang and Yoshida, 2024
, Theorem 4.6)
.
We proved the tight bound of our algorithm is
quadratic in Theorem
3.13
.
Similar to type simulations (Definition
3.8
),
Silva
et al.

(
Silva et al., 2023
)
introduce the

\mathit{XYZW}
-simulation for formulating subtyping of context free
session types inspired by

\mathit{XY}
-simulation by Aarts and Vaandrager
(
Aarts and Vaandrager, 2010
)
.
A semi-decision procedure for this problem is introduced along with an empirical analysis.
Padovani
(
Padovani, 2019
)
shows that subtyping for context-free session types is undecidable.

In asynchronous semantics, Ghilezhen
et al.

(
Ghilezan et al., 2023
)
proposed
the
precise multiparty asynchronous subtyping
(
\leqslant_{a}
)
which is operationally and denotationally sound and complete
with respect to the liveness property. The synchronous subtyping
relation
\leqslant
studied in this paper
is a strict subset of
\leqslant_{a}
.
Checking
\leqslant_{a}
is known to be

undecidable
even in the binary case
(
Lange and Yoshida, 2017
)
.
Li
et al.

(
Li et al., 2024a
)
studied the problem of subtyping in
communicating finite state machines (CFSM)
(
Brand and Zafiropulo, 1983
)

under
subprotocol fidelity
,
where the CFSMs are refined
with respect to a context of a global type
{\sf G}
.
They showed
PSPACE
-hardness to decide

monolithic refinement
(whether a set of CFSMs are valid
refinement of a given global type), and
further proposed more tractable (polynomial)
protocol refinement

where only one local type is changed at a time.
Cutner
et al.

(
Cutner et al., 2022
)
proposed a sound but not complete
approximation of
\leqslant_{a}

which uses the bounded rewriting technique
inspired by the IO-tree decomposition theorem in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2023
)
.
Their relation is integrated into
the top-down approach, and is applied to asynchronous message optimisation
in Rust.

End-Point Projections.

The end-point projection for session types
was first introduced by Honda
et al.

(
Honda et al., 2008
)
,
using the inductive plain merge
(Definition
4.6
),
with linearity conditions on channels
to check the well-formedness of global types.
Later, Bettini
et al.

(
Bettini et al., 2008
;
Coppo et al., 2015
)
proposed simplified global types
with plain merging, adopted by most works, including ours.

The inductive full merge
(Definition
4.7
)
is introduced for enlarging projectable sets of global types, see

(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
,
\mathsection
3.1,
\mathsection
8.1)
for a survey.
A recent paper
(
Yoshida and Hou, 2024
)
proves the subject reduction theorem,
communication safety,
deadlock-freedom and liveness
under the top-down approach which uses
the inductive full merge (Definition
4.7
).
The typing system incorporates the subtyping relation with the projection
(
\Delta\sqsubseteq{\sf G}
in
[
Sess-T
]
in Figure
5
).
This work has resolved misunderstanding by researchers
(caused by a misinterpretation of the claim in
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
)
that (full inductive) merging leads to type-unsafety.

Tirore
et al.

(
Tirore et al., 2023
)
defined
a projection on inductive types that is sound and complete
with respect to the coinductive projection
with plain merging. They proved its correctness in Coq.
Their algorithm relies on plain merging which
is only defined when all branches have the same projected type.
Therefore, all branches except one can be
discarded to form a candidate projection
\mathsf{trans}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}({\sf G})
(
\mathsection
4.3
). This enables its complexity to stay
quadratic (Theorem
4.22
).
They have left the
extension to full merging as their future work.
Ghilezan
et al.

(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
defined full merging on
regular coinductive types (which can be viewed as infinite trees).
We reformulate the projection in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
)
using the unfolding
function, extend to the full coinductive merging and show
its complexity is in
PSPACE
, which is
the highest among the four projections,
but produces the largest
set of local types (see Figure
4
).

Majumdar
et al.

(
Majumdar et al., 2021
)

studied a projection of
more expressive global types where a sender
can distribute messages to different participants via choice (sender-driven global types), and
proved its soundness against CFSMs.
They did not analyse the complexity of their projection.
Later, Stutz
(
Stutz, 2023
)
studied

implementability
of
those extended global types, i.e. a projection is defined if there is any combination of local specifications that respect deadlock-freedom and protocol refinement.
An
EXPSPACE
bound for checking
implementability is achieved for
0
-reachable, sender-driven global types.
Li
et al.

(
Li et al., 2024b
)
later improved their implementability algorithm
to be sound and complete with respect to
protocol fidelity and deadlock-freedom, and proved it is in

PSPACE
. Their conference paper
(
Li et al., 2023
)
claimed
their algorithm is
PSPACE
-complete, which was corrected in their full version

(
Li et al., 2024b
)
, by showing
a global type
for which the construction of an implementation
requires exponential time.
The main differences from the projections we analysed are:
(1) their global types are
more expressive

(sender-driven and asynchronous);
and (2) our projections guarantee
liveness
of local types, while
theirs guarantees
deadlock-freedom
. For example,
their algorithm implements

{\sf G}^{\prime}

in
(
Ghilezan et al., 2019
, Example 3.12)
(cf.
\mathsection
4.4
),
which generates non-live local types,
and types non-live session
M
(see Remark
6.2
).
In both their and our work,
the hardness of the implementability (i.e., projectability) remains opened.

Type Inference.

Vasconcelos and Honda
(
Vasconcelos and Honda, 1993
)

introduce a principal typing scheme
of the polyadic pi-calculus,
where types are simple tuples of types without subtyping.
Our minimum type inference system uses
constraint derivations inspired by
(
Pierce, 2002
,
\mathsection
22.3)

but needs to handle
subtyping
over selection and branching
choices, together with a combination with recursion.
These special features of session types
require graph-based formulations of the minimum types.
Scalas and Yoshida
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
propose a general typing
system, which we call the
bottom-up
approach.
The complexity of checking properties is proven
PSPACE
-complete.
Notice that in asynchronous semantics,
checking properties of typing contexts (bottom-up) is generally undecidable.

(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
did not provide
neither complexity nor a type inference system from processes.
To our best knowledge,
we have proposed the first type inference system for MPST,
and shown that its worst case complexity is exponential;
and proved the first
PSPACE
-hardness results
for checking typing context properties.

Future Work

includes an accurate comparison with implementability and subtyping relations
of asynchronous CFSMs
(
Li et al., 2024b
;
Li et al., 2024a
;
Bocchi et al., 2024
;
Bravetti et al., 2021
)
.
Our complexity result reveals that
the coinductive projection with full merging has essentially
similar complexity to the algorithm in
(
Li et al., 2024b
)
,
which produces
a larger set of local types preserving deadlock-freedom.
On the other hand, the four projections studied in this paper
yield a sound strict subset of
live
local types
under asynchronous semantics as proven in

(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
, Appendix N)
.
This projected set can be enlarged
soundly using
\leqslant
(since:
\leqslant\,\subseteq\,\leqslant_{a}
)
or decidable subsets of
\leqslant_{a}

(
Cutner et al., 2022
;
Bocchi et al., 2024
;
Bravetti et al., 2021
)

preserving liveness under the asynchronous
semantics
(
Ghilezan et al., 2023
, Theorem 6.3)
.
More exactly, the top-down methodology
replacing
\leqslant
by the appropriate (asynchronous) subtyping
in Figure
1(b)
will enlarge the typability of
MPST processes under asynchrony.
Complexity analysis of the binary asynchronous sound session
subtyping in
(
Bocchi et al., 2024
;
Bravetti et al., 2021
)

and their extensions to multiparty, as well as
comparison with
(
Li et al., 2024b
;
Li et al., 2024a
)

in terms of expressiveness and complexity are interesting future work.
Our type inference system uses general graph formats
for representing local types, which would be adaptable
for the type inference from
a flexible form of the calculus that directly uses CFSMs
as types
(
Stutz, 2024
, Chapter 6)
or the calculus
with mixed choice
(
Peters and Yoshida, 2024
)
.
Extensions to calculi with session delegations
and hiding
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
;
Honda et al., 2016
)
are challenging
future work.

EP/X015955/1 and Horizon EU TaRDIS 101093006.
