is derivable and
(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}
,
then there is a typing derivation for
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:\xi\sigma\pi

(where we define
\Gamma\sigma\pi
to apply
\sigma\pi
to each type in
\Gamma
).
Also, we prove this for sorts: if
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}

is derivable then
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\sigma\pi
.

We prove this by induction on the proof tree of the constraint derivations.

Case
[
C-End
]
. Then we can use
[
T-Inact
]
followed by
[
T-Sub
]
.

Case
[
C-Var
]
. Then we can use
[
T-Var
]
.

Case
[
C-Rec
]
. Then by the inductive hypothesis there is a typing derivation for
\Gamma\sigma\pi,\mathbf{t}:\xi\sigma\pi\vdash P:\xi\sigma\pi
. We can use
[
T-Rec
]
to get
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mu\mathbf{t}.P:\xi\sigma\pi
.

Case
[
C-In
]
. If
\sigma\pi
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
then it is also a solution of
\mathcal{C}
. By the inductive hypothesis, there is a typing derivation for
\Gamma\sigma\pi,x:\alpha\pi\vdash P:\psi\sigma\pi
. We also have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\pi\alpha}\rangle;\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant\xi\sigma\pi
. We can then do the following to create the required type derivation:

[
T-Sub
]

[
T-In
]

\Gamma\sigma\pi,x:\alpha\pi\vdash P:\psi\sigma\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).P:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S\sigma\pi}\rangle;\psi\sigma\pi

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha\pi}\rangle;\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant\xi\sigma\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).P:\xi\sigma\pi

Case
[
C-Out
]
. We have that
\sigma\pi
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
. By the inductive hypothesis, there are typing derivations for
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:\psi\sigma\pi
and
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\pi
. As
(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P:\xi\sigma\pi
. Proceed with the following derivation:

[
T-Sub
]

[
T-Out
]

\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:\xi\sigma\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha\pi}\rangle;\psi\sigma\pi

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\pi\alpha}\rangle;\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant\xi\sigma\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P:\xi\sigma\pi

Case
[
C-Bra
]
,
[
C-Sel
]
. Similar to the above: use rules
[
T-Bra
]
then
[
T-Sub
]
, and
[
T-Sel
]
then
[
T-Sub
]
respectively.

Case
[
C-Cond
]
. We have that
(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
. By the inductive hypothesis, there are typing derivations for
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:\psi_{1}\sigma\pi
,
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P^{\prime}:\psi_{2}\sigma\pi
and
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha
, with
\alpha=\mathtt{bool}\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
. Thus
\alpha\pi=\mathtt{bool}
.

[
T-Cond
]

\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:\psi_{1}\sigma\pi
\psi_{1}\sigma\pi\leqslant\xi\sigma\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:\psi\sigma\pi

\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P^{\prime}:\psi_{2}\sigma\pi
\psi_{2}\sigma\pi\leqslant\xi\sigma\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P^{\prime}:\xi\sigma\pi

\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{bool}
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{then}~P~\mathsf{else}~P^{\prime}:\sigma\pi\xi

(where
[
T-Sub
]
is used in the top subtrees).

Case
[
C-SortVar
]
. We have
x:\alpha\in\Gamma
. Therefore we can use
[
T-SortVar
]
:

[
T-SortVar
]

x:\alpha\pi\in\Gamma\sigma\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash x:\alpha\pi

Case
[
C-True
]
. We have
\mathtt{bool}=\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
. Then we can use
[
T-True
]
:

[
T-True
]

\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{true}:\alpha\pi

Case
[
C-Nat
]
. Then we can use
[
T-Nat
]
.

Case
[
C-Int
]
. Then we can use
[
T-Int
]
.

Case
[
C-Not
]
. By the inductive hypothesis, there is a typing derivation for
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{bool}
. We can use
[
T-Not
]
, noting that
\alpha\pi=\mathtt{bool}
:

[
T-Not
]

\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{bool}
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\neg\mathsf{e}:\mathtt{bool}

Case
[
C-Or
]
,
[
C-Add
]
,
[
C-Neg
]
. Similar to the above.

Case
[
C-Nondet
]
. We have that
(\sigma,\pi)
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}
, so
\alpha_{1}\pi=\alpha_{2}\pi=\beta\pi
. By the inductive hypothesis, there are typing derivations for
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}:\alpha_{1}\pi
and

\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}_{2}:\alpha_{2}\pi
. We can use the following derivation:

[
T-Nondet
]

\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}:\alpha_{1}\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}_{2}:\alpha_{2}\pi
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{e}_{2}:\beta\pi

∎

See
5.11

Proof.

We show that if
\Gamma\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
, and
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:{\sf T}_{0}
, such that
\operatorname{dom}({\sigma})\cap\mathcal{X}=\emptyset
and
\operatorname{dom}({\pi})\cap\mathcal{X}=\emptyset
,
then there exists a solution
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\xi\sigma^{\prime}\pi^{\prime}={\sf T}_{0}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\sigma
, and
\pi^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\pi
.

We also show the analogous statement for sorts,
under the same conditions as the above statement for types: if
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
, and
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:S
,
then there exists a solution
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\alpha\pi^{\prime}=S
,
\sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\sigma
, and
\pi^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\pi
.

We do this by induction on the proof tree of the type derivation. For the following sort cases, we will consider the first rule in the derivation.

Case
[
T-SortVar
]
. Then
\mathsf{e}=x
,
x:\alpha\in\Gamma\sigma\pi
. By inversion we have that
[
C-SortVar
]
was used, and so
\mathcal{C}=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{X}=\emptyset
. Take
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
and
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
. Then
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}
because
\alpha\pi=S
.

Case
[
T-True
]
. Then
\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{true}
,
S=\mathtt{bool}
. By inversion we have that
[
C-True
]
was used, and so
\mathcal{C}=\{\mathtt{bool}=\alpha\}
,
\mathcal{X}=\{\alpha\}
. Take
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma,\pi^{\prime}=\pi\cup\{\alpha\mapsto\mathtt{bool}\}
. Then
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}
because
\alpha\pi=\mathtt{bool}
.

Case
[
T-Nondet
]
. Then
\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{e}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{e}_{2}
,
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}:S
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{2}:S
. By inversion we have that
[
C-Nondet
]
was used, and so
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{1}:\alpha_{1}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}_{2}:\alpha_{2}\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathcal{X}_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\beta\}
,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\{\alpha_{1}=\beta,\alpha_{2}=\beta\}
.

By the inductive hypothesis, there exist solutions
(\sigma_{1},\pi_{1})
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
(\sigma_{2},\pi_{2})
of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
such that
\sigma_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{1}=\sigma
,
\sigma_{2}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{2}=\sigma
,
\pi_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{1}=\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{2}=\pi_{2}
,
\alpha_{1}\pi_{1}=S
and
\pi_{2}\alpha_{2}=S
. Take
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}\cup\sigma_{2},\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{1}\cup\pi_{2}\cup\cup\{\beta\mapsto S\}
. We have that
\pi^{\prime}\beta=S
as desired.

Case
[
T-False
]
,
[
T-Int
]
,
[
T-Nat
]
,
[
T-Not
]
,
[
T-Or
]
,
[
T-Add
]
,
[
T-Neg
]
. Similar to the above.

For the type cases, we will consider the first non-
[
T-Sub
]
rule used in the derivation, so the tree covered is of shape:

[
R
]

...
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:{\sf T}_{k}

{\sf T}_{k}\leqslant{\sf T}_{k-1}
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:{\sf T}_{k-1}

{\sf T}_{k-1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{k-2}
\vdots

{\sf T}_{1}\leqslant{\sf T}_{0}
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:{\sf T}_{0}

Thus we have that
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:{\sf T}_{k}
is derivable and
{\sf T}_{k}\leqslant{\sf T}_{0}
by transitivity of subtyping.

We will now consider the rule
[
R
]
used to derive
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P:{\sf T}_{k}
.

Case
[
T-Inact
]
. Then
P=\mathbf{0}
,
{\sf T}_{k}=\mathtt{end}
. By inversion we have that
[
C-End
]
was used, and so
\mathcal{C}=\{\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi\}
,
\mathcal{X}=\{\xi\}
. Take
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\cup\{\xi\mapsto{\sf T}_{0}\},\pi^{\prime}=\pi
. Then
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}
because
\mathtt{end}={\sf T}_{k}\leqslant{\sf T}_{0}
.

Case
[
T-Var
]
. Then
P=X
,
X:{\sf T}_{k}\in\Gamma\sigma\pi
. By inversion we have that
[
C-Var
]
was used, and so
\mathcal{C}=\emptyset
,
X:\xi\in\Gamma
.

Therefore
\xi\sigma\pi={\sf T}_{k}
. Take
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\cup\{\xi\mapsto{\sf T}_{0}\},\pi^{\prime}=\pi
. Then
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}
because
{\sf T}_{k}\leqslant{\sf T}_{0}
. Furthermore,
\pi^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\xi={\sf T}_{0}
as desired.

Case
[
T-Rec
]
. We can assume that no
[
T-Sub
]
rule was used directly below
[
T-Rec
]
, as we could have done the subtyping directly above instead. So
{\sf T}_{k}={\sf T}_{0}
.

Then
P=\mu X.P^{\prime}
,
\Gamma\sigma\pi,X:{\sf T}_{k}\vdash P^{\prime}:{\sf T}_{k}
. By inversion we have that
[
C-Rec
]
was used, and so
\Gamma,\mathbf{t}:\xi\vdash P^{\prime}:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}
.

By the inductive hypothesis there exists a solution
(\sigma_{1},\pi_{1})
of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\sigma_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\sigma
,
\pi_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\pi
,

\sigma_{1}\xi\pi_{1}={\sf T}_{k}
. Taking
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}
,
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{1}
works because
{\sf T}_{k}={\sf T}_{0}
.

Case
[
T-In
]
. Then
P={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?(x).P^{\prime}
,
{\sf T}_{k}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma\sigma\pi,x:S\vdash P^{\prime}:{\sf T}^{\prime}
. By inversion we have that
[
C-In
]
was used:
\Gamma,x:\alpha\vdash P^{\prime}:\psi\mid_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\xi,\alpha\}
,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\cup\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\psi\leqslant\xi\}
,
\alpha,\psi
fresh.

Let
\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}=\pi\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\alpha\mapsto S\}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,x:\alpha
. Then
\Gamma^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}\sigma^{\prime\prime}\vdash P^{\prime}:{\sf T}^{\prime}
. By the inductive hypothesis, there exists a solution
(\sigma_{1},\pi_{1})
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
such that
\sigma_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime\prime}
,
\pi_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime\prime}
,

\psi\pi_{1}\sigma_{1}={\sf T}^{\prime}
. Take
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}\cup\{\xi\mapsto{\sf T}_{0}\},\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{1}
. Then
\xi\sigma^{\prime}\pi^{\prime}={\sf T}_{0}
and
\pi^{\prime}\alpha=S
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}^{\prime}={\sf T}_{k}\leqslant{\sf T}_{0}
. Therefore
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}
as desired.

Also,
\sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=(\sigma_{1}\cup\{\xi\mapsto{\sf T}_{0}\})\setminus(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\xi,\alpha\})=\sigma_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\sigma
and
\pi^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=(\pi_{1}\cup\{\alpha\mapsto S\})\setminus(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\xi,S^{\prime}\})=\pi^{\prime\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=\pi
, as desired.

Case
[
T-Out
]
. Then
P={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle.P^{\prime}
,
{\sf T}_{k}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash P^{\prime}:{\sf T}^{\prime\prime}
and
\Gamma\sigma\pi\vdash\mathsf{e}:S
. By inversion we have that
[
C-Out
]
was used, and so
\Gamma\vdash P^{\prime}:\psi\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{e}:\alpha\mid_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\mathcal{X}_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{\xi\}
,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\psi\leqslant\xi\}
.

By the inductive hypothesis, there exist solutions
(\sigma_{1},\pi_{1})
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
(\sigma_{2},\pi_{2})
of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
such that
\sigma_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{1}=\sigma
,
\sigma_{2}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{2}=\sigma
,
\sigma_{1}\psi\pi_{1}={\sf T}^{\prime}
,
\pi_{2}\alpha=S
. Take
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}\cup\sigma_{2}\cup\{\xi\mapsto{\sf T}_{0}\}
and
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
.
\sigma^{\prime}
is a valid mapping because
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
only differ on
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
(which are disjoint due to freshness), and
\xi
is fresh.

We have that
\xi\sigma^{\prime}\pi^{\prime}={\sf T}_{0}
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{S}\rangle;{\sf T}^{\prime}={\sf T}_{k}\leqslant{\sf T}_{0}
. Therefore
(\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a solution of
\mathcal{C}
as desired. Also,
\sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=(\sigma_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{1})\cup(\sigma_{2}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{2})=\sigma
and
\pi^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}=(\pi_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{1})\cup(\pi_{2}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{2})=\pi
by freshness of
\xi,\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}
.

Case
[
T-Bra
]
,
[
T-Sel
]
,
[
T-Cond
]
. Similar to the above.
∎

D.4.
Appendix for Solving the Minimum Type

To more succinctly represent the rules for minimum types, we define the following notation, which represents all constraints with type variable
\xi
on the right hand side:

Definition D.3.

Define the
dependencies
of
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}

as
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})=\{{\sf T}_{i}\mid{\sf T}_{i}\leqslant\xi_{i}\in\mathcal{C},\ i\in I\}
.

Definition D.4.

Let
\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}_{1},\dots
range over a non-empty set of type variables. Assume

\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a minimum type graph.
We write
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}}
for a type representation
of a type graph
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v})
. We often denote

\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket
for
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}}
if
{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is clear from the context.

See
5.19

Proof.

We restate the lemma using the notation defined in Definition
D.4
.

Claim D.1.

Let
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
be a minimum type graph. Let
\pi
be a solution of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
. If
\mathfrak{v}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathfrak{v}_{3}

where
\mathfrak{v}_{3}
is a node in
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
,
then
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\pi\leqslant\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\pi
.

Each type variable in the set
\mathfrak{v}
specifies some set of behaviours that must be followed in node
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket
: thus
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket
is less constrained.
Formally, we show that
\sim\>=\{(\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\pi,\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}\!}\rrbracket\pi)\mid\mathfrak{v}\subseteq\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}\}\cup\{(\mathsf{Skip},\mathsf{Skip})\}
is a type simulation.

•

If
{\sf T}_{i}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\beta_{i}}\rangle;\xi_{i}
for all
{\sf T}_{i}
in
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})
,
then
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\alpha_{1}\pi}\rangle}\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{i}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\beta_{i}}\rangle;\xi_{i}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
,
and
\beta_{i}=\alpha_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
.
Then, as
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})
,
we have that

\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\alpha_{2}\pi}\rangle}\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{j}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\beta_{j}}\rangle;\xi_{j}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
,
and
\beta_{j}=\alpha_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
.
Thus we have
\alpha_{1}\pi=\alpha_{2}\pi=\beta_{j}\pi
for all
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\beta_{j}}\rangle;\xi_{j}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})
.
Also,
\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{i}\mid{\sf T}_{i}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\sim\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{j}\mid{\sf T}_{j}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
, so the type simulation rules are followed.

•

{\sf T}_{j}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\beta_{i}}\rangle;\xi_{j}
for all
{\sf T}_{j}
in
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})
is symmetric.

•

If
{\sf T}_{k}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{j}:\xi_{kj}\}_{j\in J_{k}}
for all

{\sf T}_{k}
in
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})
,
then
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{j}}}\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{kj}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{j}:\xi_{kj}\}_{j\in J_{k}}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
.
Thus
j\in\bigcap_{k}J_{k}
, so
for all
{\sf T}_{i}
in
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})
,
j\in J_{i}
.
Hence
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{j}}}\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{ij}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{j}:\xi_{ij}\}_{j\in J_{i}}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
.
Also,
\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{j}\mid{\sf T}_{j}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\sim\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{i}\mid{\sf T}_{i}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
.

•

If
{\sf T}_{i}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{j}:\xi_{ij}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
for all
{\sf T}_{i}
in
\mathscr{D}_{C}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})
,
then
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{j}}}\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{ij}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{j}:\xi_{ij}\}_{j\in J_{i}}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
.
Thus
j\in\bigcup J_{i}
, so
there exists
{\sf T}_{k}
in
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})
such that
j\in J_{k}
.
Hence
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{j}}}\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{kj}\mid{\sf T}_{k}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
.
Also,
\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{j}\mid{\sf T}_{j}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\sim\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{k}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{j}:\xi_{ik}\}_{j\in J_{k}}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
.

•

If
{\sf T}_{i}=\mathtt{end}
for all
{\sf T}_{i}
in
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})
, then
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}\mathsf{Skip}
. Then there exists
{\sf T}_{j}=\mathtt{end}
in
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2})
, so
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}\mathsf{Skip}
.

Thus
\sim
is a type simulation.
∎

See
5.21

Proof.

All sort constraints are satisfied because
\pi
solves
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
which is a superset of the sort constraints of
\mathcal{C}
. We will show that all type constraints are satisfied.

Case
\mathtt{end}\leqslant\xi
.
Then
\mathtt{end}\sigma\pi=\mathtt{end}
. By Definition
5.15
,

\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}
so
\xi\sigma\pi=\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\pi=\mathtt{end}
. Thus
\mathtt{end}\sigma\pi\leqslant\xi\sigma\pi
.

Case
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi\leq\psi
.
Then
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi)\sigma\pi={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha\pi}\rangle;\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\alpha\pi}\rangle}\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
.
We have
\psi\sigma\pi=\llbracket{\!\{\psi\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\beta\pi}\rangle}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\pi
,
where
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
and
\alpha=\beta\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
, so
\alpha\pi=\beta\pi
.
Thus, by Lemma
5.19
,
\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\leqslant\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket
.
So, by definition of subtyping,
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi)\sigma\pi\leqslant\psi\sigma\pi
.

Case
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}!\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\xi\leq\psi
. Symmetric.

Case
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\leq\psi
.
Then
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})\sigma\pi={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{i}\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\}_{i\in I}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{j}}}\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{j}\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
,
We have
\psi\sigma\pi=\llbracket{\!\{\psi\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{j}}}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\pi
,
where
\xi_{j}\in\mathfrak{v}
by Definition
5.15
.
Thus, by Lemma
5.19
,
\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\leqslant\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket
.
So, by definition of subtyping,
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{i}:\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})\sigma\pi\leqslant\psi\sigma\pi
.

Case
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\leq\psi
.
We have
\psi\sigma\pi=\llbracket{\!\{\psi\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{j}}}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\pi
,
where
\xi_{j}\subseteq\mathfrak{v}
by Definition
5.15
.
Thus,
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})\sigma\pi={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{i}\}\!}\rrbracket\pi\}_{i\in I}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{j}}}\llbracket{\!\{\xi_{j}\}\!}\rrbracket\pi
,
and by Lemma
5.19
,
\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\leqslant\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket
.
So, by definition of subtyping,
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\&\!\{l_{i}:\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I})\sigma\pi\leqslant\psi\sigma\pi
.

∎

We write
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}\pi
to be the graph with
\pi
applied to all transitions.

Definition D.5 (Matching simulation).

Let
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
be a minimmum type graph and
{\sf T}
be a type.
We define a simulation
\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}

between a type
represented in
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T})
as the least relation
satisfying the following rules.

[
\precsim
-Init
]

-
\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}

[
\precsim
-IO
]

\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\dagger\langle{\beta}\rangle}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\hskip 9.24994pt{\sf T}_{1}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\dagger\langle{S}\rangle}{\sf T}_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\dagger\in\{!,?\}
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}_{2}

[
\precsim
-End
]

\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}\mathsf{{\small{Skip}}}
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}\mathtt{end}

[
\precsim
-BS
]

\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\dagger{l}}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\hskip 9.24994pt{\sf T}_{1}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\dagger{l}}{\sf T}_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\dagger\in\{\oplus,\&\}
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{2}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}_{2}

Our goal is to prove that
\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}
is a type simulation, when a solution for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
is substituted to the nodes of the minimum type graph.
Here
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}^{\prime}
means “
{\sf T}^{\prime}
must respect the behaviours
of all
\psi\in\mathfrak{v}
”. Hence
\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}
means that

{\sf T}
must respect the behaviour of
\xi
, which is the type variable
corresponding to the type of
P
.

Lemma D.6.

Assume
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
.
Let
(\sigma,\pi)
be a solution to
\mathcal{C}
.
Let
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
be the minimum type graph and sort constraint
generated
by Definition
5.15
.
Let
{\sf T}=\xi\sigma\pi
.
For all
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\sf T}^{\prime}\in{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T})
, if
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}^{\prime}
then
\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
for all
\psi\in\mathfrak{v}
.

Proof.

By fixing
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\sf T}
, we write
\precsim
for

\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}
.

We prove by induction on the proof trees of
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim{\sf T}^{\prime}
.

Case
[
Sim-Init
]
. We have
\xi\sigma\pi={\sf T}
by definition.

Case
[
Sim-IO
]
. We prove the case for the input. The output
is identical. By the inductive hypothesis we have
\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}_{1}
for all
\psi
in
\mathfrak{v}_{1}
.
Let
\psi_{2}\in\mathfrak{v}_{2}
. By definition of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
, there exists
\psi_{1}\in\mathfrak{v}_{1}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha}\rangle;\psi_{2}\leqslant\psi_{1}\in\mathcal{C}
.
So
\psi_{1}\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}_{1}
and
\psi_{1}\sigma\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\alpha\pi}\rangle}\psi_{2}\sigma\pi
.
By definition of subtyping, we conclude that
\psi_{2}\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}
.

Furthermore, for all
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha^{i}}\rangle;{\psi_{2}^{i}}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1})
, we have that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha^{i}\pi}\rangle;\psi_{2}^{i}\sigma\pi\leqslant\psi_{1}\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}_{1}
for some
\psi_{1}\in\mathfrak{v}_{1}
.
Also,
\alpha^{i}=\beta\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
for all
i
.

Case
[
Sim-SB
]
. We prove the case for the selection. By the inductive hypothesis we have
\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}_{1}
for all
\psi\in\mathfrak{v}_{1}
.
Let
\psi_{2}\in\mathfrak{v}_{2}
. By definition of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
, there exists
\psi_{1}\in\mathfrak{v}_{1}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l:\psi_{2}\}\leqslant\psi_{1}\in\mathcal{C}
.
Therefore,
\psi_{1}\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}_{1}
and
\psi_{1}\sigma\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l}}\psi_{2}\sigma\pi
.
By definition of subtyping,
\psi_{2}\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}_{2}
.

Case
[
Sim-End
]
. By definition of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
, for
\psi\in\mathfrak{v}
,
\psi\leqslant\mathtt{end}\in\mathcal{C}
. Thus
\psi\sigma\pi=\mathtt{end}
.
∎

Lemma D.7.

Assume
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
.
Let
(\sigma,\pi)
be a solution to
\mathcal{C}
.
Let
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
be the minimum type graph and sort constraint
generated
by Definition
5.15
.
Let
{\sf T}=\xi\sigma\pi
.
Assume
\pi^{\prime}
is the most general solution of

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
.
Then there exists
\pi^{\prime\prime}
such that
\prec\>=\{(\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime},{\sf T}^{\prime})\mid\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}^{\prime}\}
is a type simulation.

Proof.

As
\pi^{\prime}
is a most general solution on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
, there exists
\pi^{\prime\prime}
such that
\alpha\pi=\alpha\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{S}_{\text{v}}
.
Let
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}\prec{\sf T}^{\prime}
, so
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim{\sf T}^{\prime}
. We check the conditions for type simulation:

•

If
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}
, choose an arbitrary
\psi\in\mathfrak{v}
. Then by Definition
5.15
,
\mathtt{end}\leqslant\psi\in\mathcal{C}
.
By Lemma
D.6
,
\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\mathtt{end}\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
, i.e.
{\sf T}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{end}}
.

•

If
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{\beta}\rangle}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket
, then by Definition
5.15
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}?\langle{\alpha_{i}}\rangle;\xi_{i}\leqslant\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
for all
\psi_{i}\in\mathfrak{v}_{1}
,
with
\beta=\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
.
By Lemma
D.6
,
\psi_{i}\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
{\sf T}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}?\langle{S}\rangle}{\sf T}_{1}
for some
{\sf T}_{1}
, where
S=\alpha_{i}\pi=\alpha_{i}\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}=\beta\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}

(because
\pi^{\prime}
solves
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
, which contains all sort constraints of
\mathcal{C}
).
By definition of
\precsim
,
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}\prec{\sf T}_{1}
.

•

If
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}!\langle{\beta}\rangle}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket
: symmetric.

•

If
{\sf T}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{k}}}{\sf T}_{1}
, then by Lemma
D.8
,

\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
for all
\psi\in\mathfrak{v}
. Therefore, by definition of subtyping,

\psi\sigma\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{k}}}
, so

\llbracket{\!\{\psi\}\!}\rrbracket\sigma\pi\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{k}}}
. By definition of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
,
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\&{l_{k}}}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket
for some
\mathfrak{v}_{1}
, therefore
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}\prec{\sf T}_{1}
.

•

If
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{k}}}\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket
, then by Definition
5.15
, there exists
\psi\in\mathfrak{v}_{1}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\!\oplus\!\{l_{j}:\xi_{j}\}_{j\in J}\leqslant\psi\in\mathcal{C}
such that
k\in J
and
\xi_{k}=\psi^{\prime}
.
Then by Lemma
D.6
,
\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
{\sf T}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}}\oplus{l_{k}}}{\sf T}_{1}
for some
{\sf T}_{1}
.
By definition of
\precsim
,
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}_{1}\!}\rrbracket\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}\prec{\sf T}_{1}
.

All conditions are valid and so
\prec
is a type simulation.
∎

The above two lemmas are the two statements of
Lemma
D.8
below:

Lemma D.8 (Simulation soundness).

Assume
\vdash P:\xi\mid_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathtt{tr}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
.
Let
(\sigma,\pi)
be a solution to
\mathcal{C}
.
Let
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{S}}
be the minimum type graph and sort constraint
generated
by Definition
5.15
.
Let
{\sf T}=\xi\sigma\pi
.

(a)
For all
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\sf T}^{\prime}\in{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T})
, if
\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}^{\prime}

then
\psi\sigma\pi\leqslant{\sf T}^{\prime}
for all
\psi\in\mathfrak{v}
; and

(b)
There exists
\pi^{\prime\prime}
such that

\prec\ =\{(\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime},{\sf T}^{\prime})\mid\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}^{\prime}\}

is a type simulation.

Proof.

(a) By induction on the proof tree of

\llbracket{\!\mathfrak{v}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}^{\prime}
.

(b) By verifying that the type simulation rules are satisfied, using (a).
∎

See
5.22

Proof.

\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\precsim_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf T}}{\sf T}
by
[
\precsim
-Init
]
, so by Lemma
D.8
(b),
\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\pi^{\prime}\pi^{\prime\prime}\prec{\sf T}
.
We have that
\llbracket{\!\{\xi\}\!}\rrbracket\pi^{\prime}={\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}
, and
\prec
is a type simulation, thus

{\sf T}{\text{\tiny\sf{min}}}\pi^{\prime\prime}\leqslant{\sf T}
.
∎

Appendix E
Appendix for Checking Safety, Deadlock-Freedom and Liveness

E.1.
Detailed Examples for Remark

Let us call

\Delta

weak live

defined by Definition

without

We show the weak-live context can type a
non-live
session
M
.
Consider the following context
with three parties,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}
lice,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}

(

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}:\mu\mathbf{t}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}\!\oplus\!\{\mathsf{m}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}\&\!\{\mathsf{yes}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}\!\oplus\!\mathsf{buy},\ \mathsf{no}:\mathbf{t}\}\},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}:\mu\mathbf{t}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}\&\!\{\mathsf{m}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}\!\oplus\!\mathsf{no}:\mathbf{t},\ \mathsf{cancel}\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}\&\!\{\mathsf{buy},\ \mathsf{no}\},\end{array}

\Delta^{\prime}
is neither live nor weak live.
This is because that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}
lice never chooses to send
\mathsf{cancel}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}

\mathsf{no}

to all
\mathsf{m}

The session

\Delta^{\prime}
is not live either. Now consider

\Delta
such that

,
which can be obtained by adding choices

at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}

at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}
ob
(
colored
). We let
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}})

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta^{\prime\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}:\mu\mathbf{t}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}\!\oplus\!\{\mathsf{m}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}\&\!\{\mathsf{yes}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}\!\oplus\!\mathsf{buy},\ \mathsf{no}:\mathbf{t}\},\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{cancel}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}\!\oplus\!\mathsf{no}}\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}:\mu\mathbf{t}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}\&\!\{\mathsf{m}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}\!\oplus\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{yes}},\ \mathsf{no}:\mathbf{t}\},\ \mathsf{cancel}\}\end{array}

Session

typed by

\Delta^{\prime}
is also typable by
\Delta

using the subsumption rule (
[

]
).

\Delta
is weak live but not live since there is a fair path

\Delta\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}:\mathsf{m}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}:\mathsf{no}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}:\mathsf{m}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{b}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}:\mathsf{no}}\cdots
under which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}
eller
indefinitely awaits selection from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{a}}}
lice.
Hence weak live
\Delta
types non-live
M
.

E.2.
Proof of Lemma
6.4

See
6.4

Proof.

(
\Longrightarrow
) Fix a safety property
\phi
such that
\phi(\Delta)
. By definition,
\Delta
is a safe state.
Let
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}
.
By induction on the length of the reduction sequence, then applying the second condition of safety properties, we have that
\Delta^{\prime}
is a safe state.

(
\Longleftarrow
) Define
\Delta^{\prime},\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{unf}}\Delta^{\prime},{\sf T}[\mu\mathbf{t}.{\sf T}/\mathbf{t}]
for all
\Delta^{\prime},{\sf T}
.
Note that, if
\Delta_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{unf}}\Delta_{2}
then
\Delta_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime}\iff\Delta_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime}
.
Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be a safe state for all
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}
.
Let
\phi
be the minimum relation containing
\Delta
and closed under
\rightarrow
and
\xrightarrow{\mathsf{unf}}
.

By definition, the latter two conditions for safety hold. It suffices to show that
\Delta^{\prime}
is safe for all
\phi(\Delta^{\prime})
.
Consider the shortest sequence of
\rightarrow
and
\xrightarrow{\mathsf{unf}}
reductions from
\Delta
to
\Delta^{\prime}
.
If
\Delta_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{unf}}^{*}\rightarrow\Delta_{2}
then
\Delta_{1}\rightarrow\Delta_{2}
, so the shortest sequence of reductions has the form
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{unf}}^{*}\Delta^{\prime}
.
But
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
is a safe state by the claim, and
\Delta^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime}
have the same outgoing transitions. So
\Delta^{\prime}
is a safe state. Therefore
\phi
is a safety property.
∎

E.3.
Checking Safety and Deadlock-Freedom is in PSPACE

Lemma E.1.

Let
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
. Let
\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}
, such that
\Delta^{\prime}=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}^{\prime}_{i}
. Then
{\sf T}^{\prime}_{i}\in{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T}_{i})
.

Proof.

By inspection of the typing rules of Definition
6.2
, the possible reductions of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
are exactly the possible reductions of
\mathbb{G}({\sf T}_{i})
. By Lemma
B.1
, this is a subset of
{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T}_{i})
.
∎

See
6.10

Proof.

By the above lemma, applied to each
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
. We have that
\{\Delta^{\prime}\mid\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}\}\subseteq\{\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}^{\prime}_{i}\mid{\sf T}^{\prime}_{i}\in{\rm{Sub}}({\sf T}_{i})\}
, so
|\{\Delta^{\prime}\mid\Delta\rightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}\}|\leq\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
.
∎

Algorithm 1

A nondeterministic polynomial-space algorithm for checking if a typing context is not safe. The algorithm accepts if at least one combination of nondeterministic choices results in an accepting state. The input is a context
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
.

1:

function

NotSafe
(
\Delta
)

2:

m\leftarrow\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|

3:

n\leftarrow 0

4:

\Delta^{\prime}\leftarrow\Delta

5:

loop

6:

if

\Delta^{\prime}
is not a safe state
then

7:

return
accept

8:

end

if

9:

n\leftarrow n+1

10:

if

n>m

then

11:

return
reject

12:

end

if

13:

nondeterministically choose
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
such that
\Delta^{\prime}\rightarrow\Delta^{\prime\prime}

14:

\Delta^{\prime}\leftarrow\Delta^{\prime\prime}

15:

end

loop

16:

end

function

Theorem E.2.

Checking for safety is in
PSPACE
.

Proof.

We use Algorithm
1
, which is a nondeterministic polynomial-space algorithm that accepts if and only if the input is not safe. The algorithm checks all
\Delta^{\prime}
such that
\Delta^{\prime}
is reachable from
\Delta
in
M
transitions or fewer, where
m
is given in Lemma
6.10
, accepting if there exists some
\Delta^{\prime}
that is not a safe state. As the shortest path to any state has length at most
m
, the algorithm is correct.
Furthermore, the algorithm needs to store only the current context
\Delta^{\prime}
, which is representable by type graphs in linear space, and the counter
n
, which has at most
\log{N}=\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(\sum_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|\right)
bits, so the algorithm is polynomial-space.

The result follows from
\textsc{co-NPSPACE}=\textsc{NPSPACE}
and from Savitch’s theorem:
\textsc{NPSPACE}=\textsc{PSPACE}

(
Sipser, 2013
, Theorem 8.5)
.
∎

We can use a similar algorithm for deadlock-freedom.

Theorem E.3.

Checking for deadlock-freedom is in
PSPACE
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to the proof for safety, but with the condition in line
6
replaced with
\Delta^{\prime}\not\rightarrow\implies\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in\Delta^{\prime}.{\mathsf{unfold}}({{\sf T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}})=\mathtt{end}
, which is also checkable in polynomial time.

∎

E.3.1.
A More Practical Algorithm for Safety and Deadlock-Freedom

Even though there must exist a deterministic polynomial-space algorithm for checking safety
and deadlock-freedom by the equivalence of
co-NPSPACE
and
PSPACE
, this might not be
practical due to the high time complexity of the reduction.
A more practical deterministic algorithm would do an explicit breadth-first search over all reachable states.
Even though this requires exponential memory in the worst case (so this is not a
PSPACE
-algorithm),
it takes linear time and memory in the number of reachable states.
In practice, this number is often much smaller than the theoretical bound of
\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|
.
This can also be adapted for deadlock-freedom in the same way as the other algorithm.

We instead illustrate a
deterministic algorithm that might be more practical, which instead does an explicit breadth-first search
over all reachable states (Algorithm
2
).

Algorithm 2

A deterministic algorithm for checking if a typing context is safe. The input is a context
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
.

1:

function

Safe
(
\Delta
)

2:

V\leftarrow\{\Delta\}

\triangleright
Set of found states

3:

Q\leftarrow[\Delta]

\triangleright
Queue of states to visit

4:

while

Q
is nonempty
do

5:

\Delta^{\prime},Q\leftarrow Q

6:

if

\Delta^{\prime}
is not a safe state
then

7:

return
reject

8:

end

if

9:

V\leftarrow V\cup\{\Delta^{\prime}\}

10:

for all

\Delta^{\prime\prime}
such that
\Delta^{\prime}\rightarrow\Delta^{\prime\prime}

do

11:

if

\Delta^{\prime\prime}\not\in V

then

12:

V\leftarrow V\cup\Delta^{\prime\prime}

13:

Q\leftarrow Q,\Delta^{\prime\prime}

14:

end

if

15:

end

for

16:

end

while

17:

return
accept

18:

end

function

See
6.19

Proof.

Lemma
6.10
shows that there are at most
U
reachable states from
\Delta
.
The algorithm visits each state at most once, and for each state, there are at most
\Delta
outgoing transitions,
so it checks if it is safe (or deadlock-free) in
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(|\Delta|\right)
time.
∎

E.4.
Checking Liveness is in PSPACE

See
6.14

Proof.

In Definition
6.13
, the first condition holds when the path is not live Lemma
6.15
. The second condition holds when the path is fair.
∎

See
6.16

Proof.

By considering the type graphs of
{\sf T}_{i}
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{j}
, then
\mathbb{G}({\sf T}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}})
must contain an edge
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{j}}!\langle{S}\rangle
, and
\mathbb{G}({\sf T}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{j}})
must contain an edge
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}}?\langle{S}\rangle
for some
S
.

•

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{j}:l
, then
\mathbb{G}({\sf T}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}})
must contain an edge
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{j}}\oplus{l}
, and
\mathbb{G}({\sf T}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{j}})
must contain an edge
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}}\&{l}
.

As each reduction gives rise to unique edges, and there are less than or equal to
2|{\sf T}|
edges in a type graph of
{\sf T}

(
Udomsrirungruang and Yoshida, 2024
, Lemma 4.3)
, we conclude that there are
2n
possible unique reductions.
∎

The following lemma shows that there must exist finite or periodic counterwitnesses to liveness if the context is not live. We write
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\omega}
to denote an infinite path that concatenates
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and infinitely many copies of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

See
6.17

Proof.

Fix some counterwitness
(\Delta_{i})_{i\in N}
of
\Delta
. By definition, there exists
k\in N
and
a\in\mathsf{barb}(\Delta_{k})
such that
a\not\in\bigcup_{i\geq k}\mathsf{obs}(\Delta_{i})
.

Case
N
is finite. Then we can
shortcut
some cycles:
if
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid i\geq j_{1}\}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid i\geq v\}
such that
j_{1}<j_{2}
and
k\not\in\{j_{1},j_{1}+1,\dots,j_{2}\}

then we may remove
\Delta_{j_{1}+1},\dots,\Delta_{j_{2}}
from the path.
As
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid i\geq w\}\subseteq\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\mid\exists i\geq w.\>\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\}
for all
w
, the subsets stay equal,
and the first condition is not violated because we are removing elements from
\mathsf{obs}
.
We can continue this process until there are no cycles with the same value of
|\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid i\geq j\}|
throughout.
As there are only
2n
possible values of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}
by Lemma
6.16
, and we cannot shortcut cycles containing
k
,
there are at most
2n+2
sections, each with no repeating states,
so the resulting path is of size
m
.

Case
N
is infinite. We first need to make the path periodic.
This can be done by taking the first
u\geq k
such that
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid k\leq i\leq u\}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid k\leq i\}
.
Then, take the first
u^{\prime}>u
such that
\Delta_{u^{\prime}}=\Delta_{v}
for some
v<u^{\prime}
.
For any
j
,
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid j\leq i\leq u^{\prime}\}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid j\leq i\}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\mid\exists j\leq i.\>\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\}\supseteq\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\mid\exists j\leq i\leq u^{\prime}.\>\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\}
.
But
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid j\leq i\leq u^{\prime}\}\subseteq\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\mid\exists j\leq i\leq u^{\prime}.\>\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\}
by definition, so the two sets are equal.
Also,
\bigcup_{k\leq i\leq u^{\prime}}\mathsf{obs}(\Delta_{i})\subseteq\bigcup_{k\leq i}\mathsf{obs}(\Delta_{i})
.

Thus,
\Delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\omega}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\Delta_{0},\dots,\Delta_{v-1}

and
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\Delta_{v},\dots,\Delta_{u^{\prime}-1}
is a valid counterwitness.
Then, we will shortcut cycles similarly to the finite case.
Before
\Delta_{v}
, the strategy for finite paths still works.
For the cycle
(\Delta_{v},\dots,\Delta_{u^{\prime}-1})
, we will shortcut
j_{1}<j_{2}

such that
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid j_{1}\leq i<u^{\prime}\}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{i}\mid j_{2}\leq i<u^{\prime}\}
,
by removing
\Delta_{j_{1}+1},\dots,\Delta_{j_{2}}
.
As all the removed transitions in
\{j_{1}+1,\dots,j_{2}\}
are still found in
\{j_{2},\dots,u^{\prime}-1\}
,
the second condition is not violated.
Furthermore, the first condition is not violated by the same reason as above.

Therefore, by the same reasoning as in the finite case, the resulting path is of size
m
.
∎

We can use this bound to create a polynomial-space search for exponential-length counterwitnesses.

Algorithm 3

A nondeterministic polynomial-space algorithm for checking if a typing context is not live. The algorithm accepts if at least one combination of nondeterministic choices results in an accepting state. The input is a context
\Delta=\prod_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}:{\sf T}_{i}
.

1:

function

NotLive
(
\Delta,n
)

2:

M\leftarrow(2n+2)\cdot\prod_{i\in I}|{\sf T}_{i}|

3:

U\leftarrow\emptyset

\triangleright
unfair transitions

4:

T\leftarrow\emptyset

\triangleright
observed transitions in cycle

5:

B\leftarrow\emptyset

\triangleright
barbs of
\Delta_{k}

6:

O\leftarrow\emptyset

\triangleright
observed barbs

7:

\Delta_{0}\leftarrow\Delta

8:

for

\Delta^{\prime},\mathsf{prev}(\Delta^{\prime})
in some nondeterministically chosen path
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of length
\leq M
starting at
\Delta_{0}

do

9:

U\leftarrow U\cup\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\mid\Delta^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\}

10:

U\leftarrow U\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}^{\prime}\}
where
\mathsf{prev}(\Delta^{\prime})\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}^{\prime}}\Delta^{\prime}

11:

if

\Delta_{k}
has not been set
then

12:

nondeterministically choose to set
\Delta_{k}\leftarrow\Delta^{\prime}
and
B\leftarrow\mathsf{barb}(\Delta^{\prime})

13:

else

14:

O\leftarrow O\cup\mathsf{obs}(\mathsf{prev}(\Delta^{\prime}),\Delta^{\prime})

15:

end

if

16:

end

for

17:

if

\Delta_{k}
is set and
U=\emptyset
and
B\setminus O\neq\emptyset

then

return
accept
\triangleright
finite case

18:

end

if

19:

for

\mathsf{prev}(\Delta^{\prime}),\Delta^{\prime}
in some nondeterministically chosen nonempty path
\mathcal{P}_{2}
of length
\leq M
starting at the last element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}

do

20:

T\leftarrow T\cup\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}^{\prime}\}
where
\mathsf{prev}(\Delta^{\prime})\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}^{\prime}}\Delta^{\prime}

21:

if

\Delta_{k}
has not been set
then

22:

nondeterministically choose to set
\Delta_{k}\leftarrow\Delta^{\prime}
and
B\leftarrow\mathsf{barb}(\Delta^{\prime})

23:

else

24:

O\leftarrow O\cup\mathsf{obs}(\mathsf{prev}(\Delta^{\prime}),\Delta^{\prime})

25:

end

if

26:

U\leftarrow U\cup\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\mid\Delta^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\}

27:

end

for

28:

if

\mathcal{P}_{2}
is not a cycle
then

return
reject

29:

end

if

30:

if

\Delta_{k}
is set and
U\setminus T=\emptyset
and
B\setminus O\neq\emptyset

then

return
accept
\triangleright
cycle case

31:

else

return
reject

32:

end

if

33:

end

function

See
6.18

Proof.

We use Algorithm
3
, which is a nondeterministic polynomial-space algorithm that accepts if and only if the input is not live. At a high level, the algorithm nondeterministically generates periodic paths of bounded length (Theorem
6.17
), and checks if they are counterwitnesses.
\Delta_{k}
is nondeterministically chosen as the paths are generated.

At the end of the first loop,
U
is the set of reductions
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}
such that
\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}
but there is no
\Delta_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
j>i
such that
\Delta_{j}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta_{j+1}
.
B=\mathsf{barb}(\Delta_{k})
and
O=\mathsf{obs}((\Delta_{i})_{i\geq k})
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
.
Thus we are checking if
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is a counterwitness with the given value of
k
.

At the end of the second loop, we add all reductions
\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}
to
U
and all observed transitions to
T
. After the loop, we check that all transitions were observed and not all barbs were matched, so we are checking for a counterwitness for
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\omega}
. Thus the algorithm is correct.

Furthermore, the algorithm needs to store only four polynomial-size sets
U,T,B,O
, as well as
\Delta_{k}
, and pointers to the start and end of each path, which are polynomial in size. In particular, the paths
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
do not need to be stored explicitly.
Thus the algorithm is polynomial-space.
∎

E.4.1.
A Deterministic Algorithm for Checking Liveness

We can use a similar construction to the proof of Savitch’s theorem
(
Sipser, 2013
, Theorem 8.5)

to create a deterministic algorithm for checking liveness.

See
6.20

Proof.

Define the predicate

R_{t}^{a}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T,U)
,
for barb
a
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime}
contexts,
to be true iff there exists a path of length at most
2^{t}
from
\Delta^{\prime}
to
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
such that barb
a
is never observed,
the set of observed transitions is
T
,
the set of "unfair transitions" (Algorithm
3
) is
U
.

We can compute
R_{t}^{a}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T,U)
for all possible
\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T
and
U
as follows:

•

R_{0}^{a}
can be computed for each possible transition. If
\Delta^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\Delta^{\prime\prime}
,
then
R_{t}^{a}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T,U)
iff
a\notin\mathsf{barb}(\Delta)
and
T=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\}
, and
U=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}^{\prime}|\Delta^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}^{\prime}}\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\}
,
and
R_{t}^{a}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\emptyset,\emptyset)
.
This can be done in time
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(U^{2}\cdot 2^{|\Delta|}\right)
.

•

R_{t+1}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T_{1}\cup T_{2},U^{\prime})
is true iff there exists some
\Delta^{*}
such that
R_{t}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{*},T_{1},U_{1})
and
R_{0}(\Delta^{*},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T_{2},U_{2})
, such that
U^{\prime}=U_{2}\cup(U_{1}\setminus T_{2})
.
This can be done in time
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(U^{3}\cdot 2^{2|\Delta|}\right)
.

Let
m=(2|\Delta|+2)\cdot U
be the longest possible length of a counterwitness, defined in Lemma
6.17
.
Thus, we can compute
R_{m}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T,U)
in time
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(U^{3}\log U\cdot 4^{|\Delta|}\right)
, where
t=\lceil\log m\rceil
.

Similarly, we can define
C_{t}^{a}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T,U)
to be true iff
there exists a path
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{1}}\Delta_{1}\dots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{n}}\Delta_{n}=\Delta^{\prime\prime}

of length
n\leq 2^{t}
such that barb
a
is never observed, and

T=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}_{n}\}
, and
U=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}\mid\Delta_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ell}}}\}
.
We can compute
C_{m}(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime},T,U)
in time
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(U^{3}\log U\cdot 2^{2|\Delta|}\right)
.

Then, a counterwitness exists if there exists some
a,\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}

such that
\Delta_{1}
is reachable from
\Delta
,
a\in\mathsf{barb}(\Delta_{1})
,
and
R_{t}^{a}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},T_{1},U_{1})
and
C_{t}^{a}(\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},T_{2},U_{2})
such that
U_{1}\cup U_{2}\subseteq T_{1}
,
or
R_{t}^{a}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},T_{1},\emptyset)
.

This can be done in time
\mathcal{O}\negmedspace\left(U^{3}\cdot 2^{3|\Delta|}\right)
time.
∎

E.5.
Reduction from the Quantified Boolean Formula Problem

Our construction is a typing context
\Delta_{\mathsf{init}}
that effectively computes the truth value of
\mathcal{F}
. It contains one participant for each variable (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}
) and clause (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i}
), as well as the “controller” (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}
).
Due to the constraint that selection and branching must involve a
single other participant, we build the context in a way that
allows communication in a linear topology:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}\ \longleftrightarrow\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{1}\ \longleftrightarrow\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{2}\ \longleftrightarrow\cdots\longleftrightarrow\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{n}\ \longleftrightarrow\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}_{1}\ \longleftrightarrow\cdots\longleftrightarrow\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}_{m+1}

•

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}
is the “controller” of the session: it queries the other participants to check if the QBF is true; if it is not true, it enters the undesirable state
\mathsf{T}_{\rm{bad}}
, which we will define separately for each property we check. Otherwise, the QBF is queried in an infinite loop.

•

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}
handles the truth value of
v_{i}
, and finds the truth value of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}v_{i}\dots\mathcal{Q}_{n}v_{n}\mathcal{F^{\prime}}
.
It does this by trying both values of
v_{i}
:
first, it lets
v_{i}=0
(represented by being in
state
F
in Figure
13
)
and asks
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i+1}
for the truth value of
\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}v_{i+1}\dots\mathcal{Q}_{n}v_{n}
.
During this,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}
is able to respond to requests with label
\mathsf{query}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}}
,
which are querying the truth value of the variable represented by participant
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}
.
If needed, it then lets
v_{i}=1
(represented by being in state
T
in Figure
13
)
and repeats the process.
The responses from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i+1}
are then combined (depending on
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
) into a response sent to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i-1}
: either
\mathsf{tt}
or
\mathsf{ff}
.

•

Once all
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}
have their truth values initialised,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i}
handles the truth value of the
i
-th clause of
\mathcal{F^{\prime}}
,
namely
L_{i1}\vee L_{i2}\vee L_{i3}
.
First, the participant queries the truth value from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i+1}
.
If this is false, then it immediately
returns
\mathsf{ff}
to the previous participant.
Otherwise, it queries (using
\mathsf{query}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}}
) the truth value of
each of its literals in turn, and returns the appropriate value.
An exception is
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{m+1}
, which starts the process by returning
\mathsf{ff}
.

Overview.

We reduce from the quantified Boolean formula (QBF) problem: let

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{Q}_{1}v_{1}\dots\mathcal{Q}_{n}v_{n}\mathcal{F^{\prime}},\ \mathcal{Q}_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}

be a QBF, with
\mathcal{F^{\prime}}
in conjunctive normal form:
\mathcal{F^{\prime}}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}C_{i},\ C_{i}=(L_{i1}\vee L_{i2}\vee L_{i3})
. Each
L_{ij}
is a literal, which is either a variable
v_{i}
or its negation
\neg v_{i}
. The problem of checking whether QBFs are true is well-known to be
PSPACE
-complete
(
Sipser, 2013
, Theorem 8.9)
.

Let the set of participants be
\mathbb{P}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{i}\mid i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\}\cup\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i}\mid i\in\{1,\dots,m+1\}\}\cup\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}\}
. For convenience, define
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{0}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{n+1}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{1}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{0}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{n}
.
Construct the session types in Figure
12
: let their context be
\Delta_{\mathsf{init}}=\prod_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}\in\mathbb{P}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}:{\sf T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}
.
\mathsf{T}_{\rm{bad}}
is an arbitrary type, which we will set to different types depending on the property we are checking.

We draw the type graphs of each participant in the context in Figure
13
. We label the nodes of the type graphs to more clearly prove the reduction, and we will use the names of graph nodes and their corresponding types interchangeably.

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{s}}}}}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathbf{t}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{1}!\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{p}}}}_{1}\&\!\{\mathsf{tt}:\mathbf{t},\mathsf{ff}:\mathsf{T}_{\rm{bad}}\}

\displaystyle=\mu\mathbf{t}_{1}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i-1}?\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i+1}!\langle{\mathtt{int}}\rangle;\hskip 199.16928pt(i\leq m)

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i+1}\&\!\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{query}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i-1}\!\oplus\!\left\{\mathsf{query}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{t}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i-1}\&\!\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{yes}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i+1}\!\oplus\!\{\mathsf{yes}:\mathbf{t}_{1}\},\\

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf{r}}}}_{i-1}}\oplus{\mathsf{query}_{p_{j_{i3}}}}

:
I
